# PROJECT / PROGRAMME SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 2 OF 80

### A.2 Project / Programme Executive Summary (max 300 words)

The Palestinian National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the initial national communication [INCR] to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in addition to the Palestinian Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) to the Paris Agreement, all highlight the vulnerability of Palestine to climate change with implications for all sectors. In Gaza, the population has one of the lowest per capita water availability in the world, and is already facing severe water scarcity.

Food and water insecurity will increase in the baseline scenario with a "climate contribution" to this trend, in terms of acceleration of existing anthropic factors, estimated at 30% (as will be explained further down). The project intends to counterbalance the negative compounding effect of climate change on already degraded resources. It aims to implement a new technology called Multi-Arterial Recharge (MAR) in the Gaza aquifer to create a new non-conventional water resource to be injected into the aquifer and to be reused for agricultural purposes.

The project will increase the resilience of 4,200 people involved in agriculture, and enhance the livelihood of 23,553 people, including 11,776 women; MAR will alleviate the pressure on the aquifer, thereby improving access to domestic water of 200,000 additional people.

It will also increase the production of Palestinian renewable energy, through the installation of 0.5 ha of PV scheme.

This is a huge paradigm shift for Palestine, where the MAR technology is implemented for the first time and will allow engaging directly with the nascent issue of adaptation to climate change in the area, which is the Nexus Food - Water - Energy. A map of the project area and key infrastructure is provided in Annex 2.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## C1: Strategic Context

Acute water scarcity in Palestine is a key determinant of the climate vulnerability of its population, particularly for rural areas and communities. The Palestinian National Adaptation Plan and the initial national communication to the UNFCCC highlight the importance of water resources as a critical factor affecting the country's development prospects with implications for all sectors. This is also highlighted in the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC).

The geopolitical situation substantially reduces the Palestinians' adaptive capacities, thereby compounding climate change impacts on water availability and quality. The West Bank and Gaza Strip are particularly vulnerable to the effects of climate change, especially in terms of water resources.

Palestinian water resources are among the lowest per capita available in the world, with quality far below World Health Organization (WHO) standards. The average domestic water consumption is only 72 liters per capita per day ((1/4)d) in the West Bank and 96 liters/d in the Gaza Strip [availability of drinkable quality water is deemed to be half that number].

The impacts of climate change identified at regional level or in neighboring countries broadly apply to the Palestinian situation. Studies have attempted to model the impacts of climate change on Palestine's water resources, including those by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) A1B emissions scenario [AR5], predict a decrease in precipitation and an increase in temperature over the next 30 years.

The UNDP's Water Resources Programme - UNDP's analysis of climate change impacts on Palestine highlights climate risks as a humanitarian threat. The report identifies water scarcity as one of the policy realms of disaster risk management and emergency response operations. Current high levels of food and water insecurity in Gaza and the West Bank are forecasted to increase, with the potential for significant social unrest. The agricultural sector consumes over two-thirds of water abstracted or flowing from springs in Palestine) and a fragile water supply infrastructure. This will have severe implications on the human health, social development and general well-being in the Gaza Strip.

Combining population growth forecasts and projected climate change projections, it has been estimated that Palestinian demand for water resources defined by the UNDP's Water Resources Programme (WRP), 2020, each year, around 195 Mm3 are pumped from the coastal aquifer for the needs of the population and agricultural activities (representing less than 30% of total usage), while the water annual natural recharge of this aquifer is estimated at only about 40 Mm3. This means that the demand on the coastal aquifer will increase to significant intrusion of seawater into the only freshwater resource in the area, already heavily polluted by fertilizers and other agricultural runoff.

Water availability is crucial to the food security of Palestinians, in particular in Gaza. Agriculture in Palestine is very dependent on precipitation: though irrigation is the only option in this semi-arid territory and presents a great challenge for water management, it is also the most efficient use of water resources available. Increasing aridity due to climate change (increasing temperature, decrease of precipitation, as well as sea level rise) will increase the pressure on already over-exploited aquifers, with consequences on the quality of the water (salinization and pollution) and quantity (decreased recharge and increased abstraction). The United Nations has reported that "the aquifer will irreversibly collapse by 2050 if no significant interventions towards introducing nonconventional water resource such as wastewater reuse and desalination.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (GCF) PAGE 11 OF 80

Water availability for agriculture is also related to existing irrigation and cropping patterns, which show relatively low levels of efficiency. The widespread use of submergence irrigation in the area of the project leads to overconsumption of water. The feasibility study also indicates that the cropping systems (in particular the low proportion of crops with high water requirements) are not adapted to the current climate conditions, leading to salinity of water in the aquifer, as a result of climate change (see climate change model Excel Sheet 15, Annex A), also decreases the irrigation efficiency and generates higher volumes applied for the same result.

In the context of climate change, the limited access to groundwater resources is an additional constraint. In Palestine and Gaza (there is limited perspectives of growth in the agricultural sector, in the context of high demographic and urban pressure on land in Gaza and instability of markets) depends on both securing access to sustainable water resources and improving irrigation efficiency.

For Palestine and its limited access to underground resources (because they are over exploited, for lack of adequate public infrastructure for additional mobilization) treated waste water is about the only new resource (compared with other countries). The feasibility study indicates that there is a potential for reuse of treated wastewater in the local context of irrigated agriculture to alleviate the pressure on the aquifers and therefore prevent rupture scenarios, under the increasing effects of climate change.

Making use of this resource, such as Treated Wastewater (TWW) is considered a prerequisite for any sustainable development of irrigation in Palestine, thereby increasing the profitability and enhancing climate resilience of Palestinian agriculture. 

In Palestine, only 10% of the total agricultural land area is irrigated (for example 68%, i.e., 30 Mm³) not agricultural quality requirements and were used for irrigation, it would entail a 20% increase in water available for Palestinian farmers, enough to irrigate an additional 1500 ha and create 15,000 jobs (Source: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations). The feasibility study indicates that only 30% of the TWW is already made use of this resource for irrigation (up to 80% of the TWW can be reused), this potential has not yet been exploited in Palestine, mostly due to insufficient infrastructure and capacities to effectively utilize TWW for irrigation.

Water and sanitation, drinking water and collected sewage networks and waste treatment plants (WTP) projects, are mainly funded by the Palestinian Authority (PA) (e.g., KWA, WEF, RAS), but being the main partners of the Palestinian government in this sector), but also by way of public funding. In Gaza, the North Gaza Energy Supply Project (NGESTP) is a project that aims to provide energy for water treatment plants and is up and running since March 2018, with a capacity of 36 Mm³/day (13.3 Mm³ per year). The NGEST plant will alleviate the pressure on the TWW WTP (in operation at this time) as its design capacity will also help support the treatment of sewage and water caused by the increase in population.

The following excerpt from the Baseline Study on Water Quality & Public Health in The Gaza Strip (2015, Annex 3) illustrates the acuteness and severity of the situation and actual health hazards related to water contamination in Gaza:

"It is needless to say that sewage is the largest reason for groundwater biological and chemical contamination in the Gaza Strip. The number of people living in areas where more than half of the population are served by sewerage systems has increased in the last two decades and currently reached around 80%, leaving behind only a half a million people unconnected to the sewage network and dependent on alternative means for excreta disposal.

Reports show that 19% of groundwater, 27% of domestic water samples and 29% of water network samples are microbiologically contaminated with E. coli (13%, 14% and 12% respectively) and 50% of the samples tested positive for coliform bacteria. The water situation in Gaza is dire. The Coastal Aquifer, Gaza's sole fresh water resource, is polluted by the infiltration of raw sewage from cesspits and sewage collection points and by the filtration of seawater (turbid and contaminated) through the coastal aquifer. This pollution has been exacerbated by over-extraction. The water quality will be worse in the next few years and the aquifer will not be able to cover the people water needs, where the water quality will not be able to used for any purposes (domestic, agriculture...). Testing

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (GCF)

### Consideration of Combined Concentrations of Chloride and Nitrate

It is clear that 3.6% of the domestic water is only matching with WHO drinking limit, while the remaining 96.4% is out of it.

Findings indicate that the incidence of diarrhoea as reported by health clinics is constantly increasing over the past five years, almost doubling in five years. Also, hepatitis incidence rate at or exceeding trend (207 per 100,000 population) has been observed for the last three years.

In the baseline situation, non-treated waste water is currently stored in precarious basins outside of Gaza City (population 543 thousand), and in some cases in open areas. This situation poses a serious threat to public health due to the aquifer resulting in severe contamination of groundwater of high water table (rejection of these waters to sea is not an option, for environmental and geopolitical reasons), threatening the livelihoods and health of both rural and urban populations.

The commissioning of the NEST plant offers the opportunity to increase water and food security in the Gaza Strip by generating an innovative, "new" and non-conventional resource for agricultural purposes, by infiltration in the aquifer (for groundwater recharge) or by evaporation ponds (for salt production), by reducing the pressure on existing wells, and development of an efficient irrigation scheme downstream. Infiltration of treated waste water in the aquifer will contribute to improving the quality of the groundwater and increasing its availability for agricultural purposes and, by the same token, preventing massive contamination and subsequent proliferation of water-borne diseases within the population of Gaza and Jabalia cities and their rural outlying areas.

The cycle of production and reuse of TWRR relies on an energy mix supplying the power for the functioning of the NEST WWTP; the boreholes pumping treated waste water to the evaporation ponds, and the pumps feeding the irrigation scheme at the end line. The current power comes consists of an external supply from the grid and on-site generation from emergency generators with sufficient capacity to cover the load of the facility (estimated at 9 MW). The project will also include a feasibility study for alternative energy sources and options for extending existing supply system via the distribution network are limited because of cost of fuel for the local power plants, as well as difficulties in fuel availability, in particular the limitations to increase the supply from cross-border sources.

Consequently, the Palestinian Water Authority (PWA), together with other responsible stakeholders (in particular the Ministry of Energy and Natural Resources, PENGAS) identified the most viable long-term sustainable power supply option for the NEST facilities during its whole life-cycle, by combining different sources of energy (electric grid, solar, wind, biogas, diesel), reducing greenhouse gas emissions (see attached document).

Closing the water cycle, by the reuse of treated waste, calls for increased coordination between several stakeholders including the Palestinian Ministry of Agriculture, in cooperation with PWA and EQA (Environment Quality Authority), and the farmers benefiting from this project. Clear and careful monitoring of water quality of water used for irrigation or for recharge the aquifer will be guaranteed. Enforcing regulations for the use of treated water and monitoring of the quality and quantity of water in the aquifer will be key challenges of the project...

# C2 Project: Program Objective against Baseline

## The Gaza Ground Aquifer is the only source of fresh water for the population of the Gaza Strip. This aquifer serves mainly as a source of domestic water and for irrigation of agricultural crops.

### With one of the world's highest demographic density (around 500 people/km²) and population growth (around 3%/year), the demand on this resource is increasing, while its recharge rate remains very low (1.2% of total annual recharged volume), leaving the population in an extreme state of vulnerability (domestic water availability is already under the 100 l/cap/day threshold as defined by the WHO) if this trend was to be continued.

### Climate change will have a significant impact on the Gaza Ground Aquifer, and it is expected that, according to the worst-case scenario, with regard to the evolution of the situation of the water balance in Gaza and the future of its availability for domestic and agricultural uses, the following indicators intend to model the baseline scenario with regard to water scarcity and seek out the climate component of the growing vulnerability (health and economic) due to lack of water:

| Indicator | Formula |
| --- | --- |
| SWBU/Δt = 1 / ΔAP + ΔEETP (Ag+Ag+) + ΔEffirr (i.e. ΔDEM + ΔAG + ΔInd) |

Where ΔWB/Δt is the evolution of the "usable" water balance (i.e. potable and usable water not counted as positive quantity in the Aquifer Water Balance).

### In a context of relatively high uncertainty at regional and local level, a no-regrets approach calls for the adoption of medium to worst-case scenarios for 2050 (Δt = [2018 - 2050]), which are detailed in the Excel sheet in Annex A. The following table summarizes the evolution of each indicator over Δt and provides an estimate of how it characterizes the contribution of each factor to the global trend of water scarcity in Gaza, as follows:

| Indicator | Evolution |
| --- | --- |
| ΔP | The evolution of precipitations over Δt as a result of climate change. Rainfall is the only significant factor contributing to the increase in precipitation (from 500 mm/year currently to an average of 620 mm/year by 2050). This will lead to a decrease in the recharge from 60 l/m²/y to 31 m³/y as a result of climate change. |
| ΔEETP (Ag+Ag+) | The evolution of crop demand for water (evapotranspiration) of the existing agricultural area and of its variation on Δt (i.e. ΔAg), as a result of increasing temperatures due to climate change. This will lead to an increase in evapotranspiration over Δt, which is expected to be 10% higher than today for the Gaza Strip over Δt, which will cause an increase in crop demand for water (EETP) between 10 and 19% (see column "ΔAg" of Annex A). This will lead to a decrease in the recharge from 60 l/m²/y currently to an average of increase in crop water requirement (+ 15%) is applied to the total cultivated area in Gaza (10,000 ha) because: (i) for irrigated crops, this coefficient is applied only to the increased cultivated area; and (ii) for rain-fed crops, it will mean less recharge of the aquifer. Total crop demand for water over Δt is expected from 140 to 160 Mm³/y as a result of climate change. |
| ΔEffirr | Represents the loss in efficiency of irrigation due to increasing salinity of the aquifer, as a result of the increase in evapotranspiration (see above). According to predictions for 2050 will further salinity intrusions in the aquifer which will, in turn, degrade its quality from moderately saline (600 - 1,000 mg/l) to highly saline (3,000-4,000 mg/l), which is expected to reduce irrigation efficiency for agricultural purpose (more water needs to be applied to maintain the same yields and locking practices are required) by an estimated 25% with regard to baseline. This coefficient is applied only to irrigated areas and generally leads to a decrease in recharge from 60 l/m²/y currently to 45 l/m²/y as a result of climate change. |
| ΔDEM | The evolution of domestic water consumption over Δt. Population is expected to double over Δt and it is assumed that its domestic water consumption, bringing it to 200 Mm³/y in 2050. |
| ΔAg | The evolution of water usages in agriculture due to an increase in cultivated areas or intensification of agricultural practices (see above). This coefficient is applied only to the increased cultivated area and generally leads to a decrease in recharge from 60 l/m²/y currently to 45 l/m²/y as a result of climate change. |
| ΔInd | The evolution of water usages in the industrial sector over Δt, which is also deemed negligible in the case of Gaza and its stunted industrial development. |

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal), Page 14 of 90

In aggregating these factors together, it is possible to segregate the "Human" component (12/100m³ + ΔAq + ΔH(1)), contributing a negligible 100 m³/y to the water scarcity trend, from the "Climate" component (1/MUDP + ΔTPTP (Ag+ΔAg) + ΔEFT (Iril)), contributing a negative 42 Mm³/y to increased water vulnerability for the population of Gaza over the course of the next 30 years. Hence, 16% of the overall determinant of change in the baseline scenario.

The project aims at limiting the compounding and amplifying effects of climate change on an already degraded security situation in Gaza Strip. The project will be implemented through a combination of two components: (i) reuse of treated waste water for irrigated agriculture; this will enhance the resilience of agriculture and related livelihoods in the Northern part of Gaza and, at the same time, alleviate the pressure on the aquifer and free equivalent water for other uses such as domestic consumption. The project will provide a treatment capacity of 13 million cubic meters per year (MCM/y) of treated waste water which will be injected into the aquifer, thanks to the infrastructure provided through the project; (ii) an additional storage capacity of 20 MCM/y in the Gaza Strip, covering over 95% of its needs. In a closed system, as that of the Gaza aquifer, where every drop is needed and used, this additional amount secured for agriculture will free the equivalent amount for other uses, in particular domestic consumption.

The project will deliver infrastructures immediately downstream from the NGEST WWTP which will allow the recharge of the aquifer using treated waste water (13 MCM/year), its recovery by a network of wells, its storage and its reuse for irrigation purposes (1500 ha = 1-1500 ha of gross irrigated area; the net area is estimated at 1200 hectares) in the vicinity of the WWTP.

The project has three main outputs and its theory of change is described in Annex S. The technical and feasibility studies implemented for the preparation of the project have been conducted over a period of 5 years due to "stop go" in the context of intermittent degradation of the security conditions in Gaza. AFAI and EISA/ESMIP/RAP are involved as consultants in the preparation of this project (related to various irrigation schemes and EISA/ESMIP/RAP related to these infrastructures); the 2017 version of the design study is

## Input 1.1.1 Rehabilitation of 7 infiltration basins for recharge of the aquifer

The belated entry in service of the NGEST WWTP (in 2018 instead of 2015), the dangerous accumulation of raw water, and the lack of a proper treatment plant have constrained the rehabilitation of the 7 infiltration basins in the context of Gaza. This has constrained Palestine in transferring the waste water from the Beit Lahia WWTP to the infiltration basin located in the NGEST perimeter (and it is planned that this will be done in 2019). The raw water has been infiltrated in the aquifer, creating a pollution plume underground and also accumulating residue and clogging the lateral pipes.

The World Bank (WB) will undertake to rehabilitate 2 of the 7 basins and the project will complete the works (rehabilitation) that remain as barrier to deliver a fully operational infiltration scheme for the recharge of the aquifer. Since this input is 100% from AFD grant funding, tender documents have been published in spring 2019 and contractor is currently being selected.

## Input 1.1.2 Delivery of the recovery scheme

The WWTP will deliver a high-quality water treatment obtained by infiltration through clay soil and the recovery of the water, stored in the shallow section of the aquifer, by a network of 28 wells located 500 m downstream from the infiltration point in a concentric formation. The recovered water will converge, through a network of pipes connecting to the NGEST perimeter, into a single well (the recovery well) and then be conveyed to the NGEST perimeter.

The organization of the full NGEST scheme is presented in the final design study presented in Annex 5. Technical specifications for the main output of the design study as well as precise location of wells, which are also presented in the ESA and RAP.

The first part of this investment (14 recovery wells and 1 reservoir) has been completed with UNF financing: the project contract was signed on March 2019. The second part is still under preparation (6 recovery wells and 1 reservoir).

## Activity 1.2 Water – Energy Nexus, development of renewable low-emission energy solutions

## Input 1.2.1 Development of solar energy capacity for the NGEST WWTP and treated waste water (TWW) reuse complex

Securing the treatment of waste water, the recovery of the clean water from the aquifer and its transfer to agricultural lands depends on the performance of the NGEST plant and of the Recovery Scheme (RS) and, in particular, on the reliability of its power supply (strongly constrained in the baseline by restrictions on access to electricity). The project will therefore develop a solar energy capacity for the NGEST WWTP and TWW reuse complex.

### OUTPUT 2: DEVELOPMENT OF IRRIGATION, WATER EFFICIENCY AND CLIMATE RESILIENT AGRICULTURE

#### Main results from this output:
- Agricultural farms and livelihoods are maintained in Northern Gaza.
- Improved agricultural practices and more efficient use of water are extended to increase the resilience of agriculture to the effects of climate change and implementing climate resilient agricultural practices.

This output consists of the delivery of an irrigation scheme over a gross irrigable area of 1,500 ha as well as services for the rehabilitation of existing infrastructure. The project will contribute significantly towards increasing the resilience and reducing their vulnerabilities. The beneficiaries are all the households end-users of the water that will be delivered by the irrigation scheme. They are already identified and aware of the project.

#### Activity 2.1: Improved water service for irrigation is brought to 4,200 beneficiaries over 1,500 ha

**Input 2.1:** Delivery of an irrigation system, 1,500 ha


---
**Page 23**
---

The project will deliver a distribution network in order to service two irrigated salt-affected areas of 500 ha and 1,000 ha of pipe and the upgrading of the booster station in order to support drip irrigation practices. Project areas were selected based on their potential for water use efficiency and the willingness of farmers to participate, on which farmers are present in the baseline. On-farm investments will be included in the costs supported by the project but will be reimbursed (with a subsidy rate) by the farmers over a 3-year period in order to set up an initial farm-level buffer against future price increases. The project will also provide for the construction of 1,000 ha plus one for the provision of on-farm equipment (drip irrigation). Tendering documents for the first lot of tenders are available at the website of the Green Climate Fund and AGF grant financing.

Also are tendering documents for the Works supervision contract associated to these works. The NGFF WWT, which is operating since March 2018, generates 356,600 m³/day of treated water, i.e., 13 million m³/year. This water is used by farmers in Gaza for irrigation and other purposes such as livestock watering (horticulture and vegetable growing) in Gaza. In the current situation, pumping in the aquifer is done through private wells and boreholes, which are owned by shareholders who have access to the water and pay a fee to the shareholders who are the main users of the water and pay the real cost of its extraction (mainly the cost of diesel, O&M); non-shareholders may have access to water, but will pay an additional fee for apart from the cost of extraction.

In case of the NGFF WWT, the project will provide a distribution network in order to service two irrigated salt-affected areas of 500 ha and 1,000 ha of pipe and the upgrading of the booster station in order to support drip irrigation practices. Project areas were selected based on their potential for water use efficiency and the willingness of farmers to participate, on which farmers are present in the baseline. On-farm investments will be included in the costs supported by the project but will be reimbursed (with a subsidy rate) by the farmers over a 3-year period in order to set up an initial farm-level buffer against future price increases. The project will also provide for the creation of a Water Users Association (WUA), to which the recovered water will be delivered and sold to and which will have the responsibility of equally distributing it to all users through the application of drip irrigation systems.

The WUA will be responsible for the maintenance of the distribution network, as well as for providing the project with the necessary infrastructure. The WUA will also be responsible for ensuring that the recovered water is distributed fairly among its members and that they have access to the water at any time. The WUA will allow availability of water for all farmers, every day, 12 hours per day. The drip irrigation systems installed by the project will increase farm productivity and generate more crops for the drip and consequently generate more income for the farmers.

Activity 2: Increased climate resilience of agriculture, adaptation of cropping systems to climate change

Input 2.2.1: Extension services to farmers

In the baseline situation, farmers have little access to extension and advisory services and in the context of limited and fluctuating access to water, cropping patterns practiced by farmers show low levels of profitability and efficiency. The project will provide a range of services to farmers that has been shown how changes and transformations at farm level can improve the profitability of agriculture and save water.

Nevertheless, these changes cannot be imposed on the farmers and will need to be adapted to the needs on a case by case basis. The project will provide extension services through the following components: technical (technical and economic advisory; capacity building by demonstrations; farmer field schools; training).

The preliminary data obtained through hydrological modeling, as well as analysis of the quality of water extracted from the NGFF WWT, shows that the recovered water is suitable for irrigation in the project areas. In Palestine law, the recovered water is equivalent to groundwater, on which no restrictions are imposed (contrary to treated waste water coming directly from the treatment plant and used for irrigation). The high level of nitrogen in the water extracted from the NGFF WWT will be managed by the project through the application of appropriate amounts of fertilizer).

Finally, a new approach based on disinfected effluent sludge from the NGFF WWT will be promoted by the project in order to substitute the use of chemical fertilizers for environmental and economic reasons (in line with Palestinian standards and EQA approval).

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Input 2.2: Table of on-farm water saving equipment

The provision of the above technical extension services is an opportunity for Gazan farmers to transform their practices and the ways they manage water at on-farm level in order to adapt to water scarcity and compounding effects of climate change. These transformations nevertheless require a concomitant mix of services to farmers and provisions of appropriate technologies. The following table lists some of these technologies:

### Surface irrigation (based on canals which are dug by the farmers or workers) typically requires no equipment and only relies on gravity flow, but it is highly prone to water losses in canals, to levels as:
1. Evaporation from the water surface
2. Deep percolation to soil layers underneath the canals
3. Leaking through the banks of the canals
4. Overtopping the bunds
5. Runoff over the bunds
6. Runoff in the drain
7. Rat holes in the canal bunds

As shown in the figure below (source: FAO):

| Irrigation methods | Field efficiency |
|--------------------|-----------------|
| Surface irrigation (border, furrow basis) | 50%             |
| Sprinkler irrigation | 75%             |

Surface irrigation is widespread in Gaza, as it is in most low-income countries where irrigation is practiced. But its low efficiency is one of the main obstacles to adaptation of the farming system to growing water scarcity because of climate change. Other irrigation methods exist, which show greater water efficiency (see table below), but require specific equipment.


---
**Page 25**

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 20 OF 80

### Drip irrigation (Source: FAO) 90%

A typical drip irrigation system is, according to the FAO, generally composed of the following components:

- The pump unit takes water from the source and provides the right pressure for delivery into the pipe system.
- The control head consists of valves to control the discharge and pressure in the entire system. It may also have a filter to remove fine material suspended in the water. Some control head units contain a fertilizer or nutrient tank. These tanks allow the application of nutrients into the water during irrigation. This is one of the major advantages of drip irrigation over other methods.
- Mainlines, submainlines and laterals supply water from the control head into the fields. They are usually made of PE (polyethylene) pipe with a diameter ranging between 15 mm to 70 mm depending on the size of the field when exposed to direct solar radiation. Lateral pipes are usually 13-32 mm diameter.
- Emitter lines supply water from mainlines, submainlines and laterals into the plants. They are usually spaced more than 1 meter apart with one or more emitters used for a single plant such as a tree. For new crops more closely spaced emitters may be used to wet a strip of soil. Many different emitter designs exist but they all have in common that they provide a specified constant discharge which does not vary much with pressure changes, and does not block easily.

| Emitter lines | Mainlines | Submainlines |
| --- | --- | --- |

Conversion to drip irrigation combined with the appropriate capacity building of farmers and adaptation of the cropping systems (which the extension services provided by the FAO will ensure) can divide by 2 or 3 the water losses in Gaza, and is therefore a key adaptive measure to tackle compounding effects of climate change on water scarcity.

Nevertheless, investment by the farmers in these technologies (widely spread in Jordan) and appropriate training and extension services are required. This is hampered by the high cost of the appropriate equipment (estimated at 3 000 ESR/ha) and the relatively low profitability of the crops grown under drip irrigation.

Observation of similar contexts in Israel, Jordan or North Africa shows that promoting water efficient irrigation in Gaza corresponds to a key adaptive measure to adaptation to climate change, as it appears relevant and necessary in all future scenarios, including the present situation.

The project intends to provide a "push" towards conversion of current irrigation practices to more efficient ones (sprinkling and drip) by financially supporting it, through the pre-financing of 100% of the cost of the equipment needed for it to happen (along with training and extension services mentioned above). This pre-financing will be delivered in two phases: a first phase of pre-financing of 50% of the total cost of the equipment, which will be agreed to reimburse the amount invested by the project for on-farm equipment, over a period of 5 years. The proceeds of these reimbursements will serve to constitute the WUA's Operation and Maintenance (O&M) and maintenance fund.

## Input 2.2.3: Gender, land tenure and irrigation = a gender-sensitive approach to agricultural resilience to climate change

A Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been developed and is presented in Annex 12. The GAP will be the overall guiding document for the mainstreaming of gender in relation with all outputs of the project. The main specific inputs related to this are:

- **Land tenure**: In Gaza, land tenure is more in favor of men, as a result of cultural practices, social customs and traditions. This reality is strongly embedded in a relatively rigid and complex cultural apparatus, which the project will have no power to change.

In relation specifically with the development of irrigation in the area, the project will invests (through an initial diagnostic during inception phase) opportunities whereby women could gain access to the land they own or improve their access to land that is not owned by them but where they have a right to use it. The opportunities will be sought through a process of community involvement, whereby family and community members will be involved in identifying the women who need improved access to land; of course, the first step of the community involvement around the issue of "irrigation and gender" should not be to focus on irrigation in the communities, in order to understand their needs objectives.

Through a process of awareness raising and capacity building, it is anticipated that a group of women willing to embark on a process of improved access to land and water will be formed, sponsored by the community. This group of women will be supported by the project (namely through a methodology developed by the FAO), that will be adapted for the specific context of Gaza. The support provided will include financial resources, technical participation of women to the governance of the WUA and technical services in order to develop intensive and quality irrigation systems.

As a second step, if possible/needed/wished, groups of women will be supported to form into cooperatives in order to market their products or create food banks for their families, according to their objectives. This activity will be coordinated by the team of the Agroforestry Project, which will involve local civil society and women's organizations.

In terms of methodology, the GAP will be implemented using inputs from the FAO (see Annex 10) and particularly from the International Union for Conservation of Nature - IUCN's training manual on gender and climate change. NGOs and WNGOs will not be EEs. They may be recruited or selected to support the implementation of this input.

## Output 3: MANAGEMENT OF THE WATER CYCLE AND CAPACITY BUILDING OF STAKEHOLDERS

Main expected results from this output:

- Improved water use (co-management with users) and increased water use efficiency in the context of growing aridity and increased demand due to climate change;
- Sustained operation and maintenance of adaptation infrastructure.

This output represents the institutional aspect of the project, which, in the context of "reuse of waste water" and its multiple stakeholders and need for coordinated actions, is of particular importance.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Activity 3.1: Strengthening PWA in its role of coordination and quality control of the process of reuse of treated water

### Input 3.1.1: Technical assistance

The PWA is the main implementing agency of the project and will be responsible for all the infrastructures that will be developed under the project, i.e., the establishment of the irrigation scheme (in particular the on-farm investments) and the "soft" components. It will coordinate with the ministry of agriculture and the FAO (see Annex C).

A technical assistance TA will be provided to the PWA for the duration of the project. The TA has been designed with the PWA in order to ensure progressive transfer of tools and know-how within the project time frame of five years. Indeed, the PMU is well staffed and has already benefited from several capacity building activities since the bid/award process (see Annex C). Main issues needing support identified by PWA are:

- Capacity building of the implementers: MoA/FAD and FAO
- Setting-up the MSEP for water quality for irrigation and for the whole project implementation (including coordination with MoA/FAD)
- Stakeholder engagement, including promoting the MSEP and coordinating messages to different stakeholders (including beneficiaries).
- Some technical assistance will be provided on an ad hoc basis, such as ESMF and RAP implementation, delivery of on-farm irrigation equipment, Gender action plan implementation.

In the context of Palestine and at the high professional level of the PWA, there is no need for full time TA for the duration of the project. The TA will be provided in a phased manner with a full-time presence during the first two years and gradually be converted from full-time into backstopping support and short term expertise on an ad hoc basis, for the remaining duration of the project.

This TA will be provided to the PMU at the PWA in Ramallah and Gaza. The corresponding services will be acquired through an international bidding process.

Furthermore, the PMU will be supported in Works supervision by specialized contractors.

Tendering procedures for Technical Assistance are currently being prepared by PWA (the activity is 100% under AID grant financing).

### Input 3.1.2: Setting-up of a water quality monitoring and control system

The PWA, in coordination with FAO, will be in charge of establishing and implementing monitoring procedures to supervise the quality of the water delivered by the WTPs to the irrigation scheme through the wells downstream in order to guarantee the quality of the water delivered to the irrigation scheme. The system will rely on 15 monitoring wells equipped with a groundwater quality monitoring system (GQMS) installed at the outlet of each well, including the upgrading of the laboratory located at the NGEST WTP and the capacity building of the staff employed in the laboratory in charge of performing the quality tests.

The monitoring results will be used to inform the ESMF of the project and will provide the information and data necessary to sustain a quality and transparent communication and dialogue between the water provider and the end users, as well as to support agricultural practices and cropping patterns to quality and quantity of the water delivered to the irrigation scheme.

A Groundwater Quality Monitoring Plan will be put into place, based on the Palestinian/Jordanian guidelines for irrigation water quality (see Annex B.1) and in accordance with the World Health Organization (WHO) standards in order to follow a zero risk policy. These standards and the full Groundwater Quality Monitoring Plan are presented in Annex 23. The Water Quality Monitoring Plan will pay special attention to the most hazardous contaminants for HA, such as nitrates, nitrites, pesticides, heavy metals, etc., which will be regularly tested in the water delivered by the WTPs. These parameters will also be periodically tested in the soil in order to control accumulation. If soil samples present values exceeding internationally recognized standards, boron and mercury shall also be tested in plants to control absorption, bioaccumulation and possible health hazards for consumers.

The laboratory and related equipment to be financed through the project will increase the monitoring autonomy of PWA and allow thorough and intensified monitoring of water quality for the duration of the project, prior to and during the activation of the recovery wells and distribution of water to farmers. Once the laboratory is up and running, it will be handed over to the WUA.

As per the 2014 Water Law and recently approved by-laws on the Water Users Associations (2018, see Annex A3), the project envisages that the operation and management of the Reuse and Recovery Systems should, when possible, be transferred to full management by the users. This co-management or co-operation has both an institutional (establishment and capacity building of a WUA) and financial dimension (cost-sharing of investments and O&M costs). The WUA will also have responsibility for the maintenance and a water tariff at certain tariffs and for a set of rules and procedures for the management of the infrastructure.

Several co-management scenarios exist and determining the appropriate one in the context of this project is the result of a careful analysis of the “capacity” (i.e. ability to manage) and “willingness to pay” study that was undertaken, in the first few months of the project with the support of the FAO (see Annex 10), locally with the farmers/communities and nationally with the representatives of the irrigation sector.

A possible scenario, that will necessarily have to be defined and adapted through the process described above, could be as follows:

### The WUA would own (and for the first two years, also operate) the Recovery and Reuse Systems with the ultimate goal of transferring the operation and management of the irrigation scheme to the Water Users Association (WUA):

#### 2. What this might imply:
- PWA will own the Reuse System, and operate it for the first three years of the project;
    - During the first three years the WUA and Ministry of Agriculture in addition at PWA would receive intense co-operation.
- After the first three years of the project, PWA will continue operate the recovery scheme until the functional establishment of the National Water Company (NWC) and the WUA would assume operation and management responsibilities.
- PWA would continue to own the irrigation scheme but would lease them to the WUA.

#### 3. The farmers will own and be responsible for operation of the On-Farm System (drop irrigation network), with the support of the WUA, in terms of technical assistance and capacity building with modern irrigation techniques and the proposed cropping pattern.
- In financial terms, the sustainability and replicability of the project depends on the involvement of various donors, government agencies and farmers. The following costs will be incurred:
    - Capital Investment for the Water Recovery Scheme;
    - Capital Investment for the Water Reuse (Irrigation) scheme up to Farm's Gate;
    - O&M Costs for the Water Recovery (Injection) Scheme;
    - O&M Costs for the Water Reuse (Injection) Scheme;
    - Capital Investment for On-Farm System.

The preferred scenario involves Capital (1), 2 and 5 and O&M subsidies from the Government, in a context of high vulnerability. The cost of the recovery wells will be paid by the Government/Donors. Costs (3) and (4) would be subsidized by the Government only until Farmers have paid back cost (5). Farmers are expected to pay for the development of their own farm. All other costs are paid by the Government/Donors for the first 3 years (i.e. the time it takes to recover the investment). After this period, the WUA will be responsible for paying O&M costs for the whole system (evaluated tariff at ~33 USD/m³ and would need validation by the Water Sector Regulatory Board, WSRC, as per the 2014 water law).

### Input 3.2.1: Identification and establishment of the co-management scheme - Structuring and capacity building of a WUA

Any scenario or co-management to be established during the lifetime of the project implies that:
- The WUA to be created will be rapidly functional and have the adequate level of capacities to dialogue with the water users, including women and men, on both financial and non-financial conditions whereby effective co-management and cost-sharing will enter into force.
- The farms, constituting the economic entities using the water, have been sufficiently supported by the project in terms of capacity building and access to credit facilities. This is necessary in order to create the necessary resources and incentives to participate in the costs of O&M of the scheme.

Setting-up and support to WUAs will be handled by the ministry of the Ministry of Agriculture for Palestine (for the purpose of this project, it will be referred to as the MOA) or through the FAO, its implementation partner, through which other institutions and NGOs may also be involved.

### Input 3.2.2: Integration of women in the governance bodies of the WUA

In order to ensure that women are fully integrated into the management of land and water, it is essential that positive action be taken to ensure that women are represented in the bureau of the WUA and have effective means of taking part in the decision-making with regard to water allocations.

### Activity 3.1: Communities are empowered through support

#### Input 3.2.1: Identification and establishment of the co-management scheme - Structuring and capacity building of a WUA
As is the case in other parts of Palestine, in particular in occupied areas of the West Bank, the protracted conflict situation has consequences on the state of mind and psychological welfare of the populations, affecting negatively human relations within families, between neighbors, and between communities and households. In turn, this situation has implications, in the project area, on the capacity of farmers to make use of their land and to work together in community-based or collective water management (WUA) and agricultural (cooperative) structures.

This situation and its complexity will be analyzed during inception by the FAO in order to understand the determinants of maximizing the irrigated area and agricultural production. This diagnostic study will involve household surveys for women and men, including focus groups and analyses of their situation.

#### Input 3.2.2: Psychosocial support and strengthening of community and household bonds
As suggested above, transfer to users of hydraulic infrastructure and community-based organizations will have a chance to work and perform only if the women and men that constitute them are capable of interacting positively towards each other in an environment where social cohesion is weak or non-existent. This implies that change or change in terms of gender will not take place spontaneously. This is particularly the case in the context of Gaza and when a common pooled resource is at stake. Community and collective work leading to these changes requires changes in attitudes, which are difficult to achieve without psychosocial support. This type of support is then catalytic and quintessential to successful adaptive strategies.

In the community-based phase, and based on a more detailed baseline study to be performed during the inception phase, the project will design and deliver a psychosocial support package to the communities including collective and individual services aimed at re-balancing the community dynamics. A particular set of services and activities will target women and men in order to address their specific needs as described above. This activity will therefore be strategic for and closely linked to the implementation of the Gender Action Plan.

This support will be delivered through the FAO, which will mobilize other specialized organizations.

# Background Information on Project / Programme Sponsor (Executing Entity)

The PWA is a public entity directly attached to the Presidency of the Palestinian Authority; its mission is to manage, develop and protect water resources for Palestinian citizens, while respecting the environment.

According to the Water Sector Reform Strategy (WSRS), the PWA has been designated as the lead agency in conducting the reform process according to a strategic three-year plan. The plans involve establishing a National Water Company to manage all water resources, a Regional Water Utilities institution to efficiently manage and operate water utilities, and a Water Resources Authority to regulate water use, which will be responsible for managing the water irrigation infrastructure.

It is important to note that the law by-law for Water (Law No 10 of 2005) was amended in 2013. The PWA has headquarters in Ramallah and Gaza, as well as representation in all governorates. A Project Management Unit (PMU) was set up in 1997 within the PWA to manage all projects implementation financed by donors or international organizations. The PMU is a multi-disciplinary team of professionals, including 11 engineers (mechanical, civil, environmental, water, and wastewater), and one Financial Manager for the implementation of NGST WWTP and the first batch of the Recovery Scheme. The PMU in the Ramallah office includes five members who are directly responsible to the PWA Chairman. The PMU works with other PWA departments (Procurement Department and Financial Controllers within the Palestinian Ministry of Finance) as well as international donors, including the World Bank, which has been supporting the World Bank, which helps it streamline its financial and procurement processes to satisfy both the Palestinian procedures as well as international donors' reporting requirements. Several major projects where implemented by the PWA with the support of the World Bank include: the Wastewater Treatment Plant at Al-Bireh (2005), the Wastewater Treatment Plant at Ramallah (2013) and the Wastewater Treatment Plant at Jenin (2017). The PMU will form the core team for the future National Water Company.

Thanks to comprehensive working sessions between the PWA/PMU and the World Bank, the PWA/PMU team has been able to develop a detailed plan of action for tendering activities for capital investments, recruiting international technical assistance for project management support, and for ensuring the financial management of the Project. The Chairman of the PWA has officially appointed each member of the PMU as part of this plan.

This team will be responsible for the operation and maintenance of NGST WWTP and the first tranche of the Recovery Scheme, as well as the implementation of the Project. As a result, salaries and operational costs of the PMU will be covered by the Project, as presented in the table below. The remainder will be financed by the World Bank budget support.

Furthermore, the PWA will prepare a note describing the institutional set-up for O&M of all NGST facilities (WWTP, WWTPs, and Recovery Scheme). This note will also include an estimate of the required financial contribution to the Project. This note shall include a cost analysis of O&M including: fixed costs (e.g., labor) and variable costs (e.g., energy), as well as provisions for renewal of the irrigation scheme components (pipes, pumps...), and a discussion of how the costs will be recovered through different scenarios involving the

For activities located outside the mandate of the PWA, related to on-farm activities, the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) of the Palestinian Authority will take over the implementation as an Executing Entity. The Ministry structure is such that it has three directors for agriculture and the MoA has a number of district offices that are providing services to the framers within their district offices. However, within the particular context of the Gaza Strip, the MoA designated three directors in Gaza strip to coordinate with PWA for this project implementation. The MoA will be responsible for the on-farm activities and will provide technical support to implement Outputs 2.2, 3.2 and 3.3 based on the FAO know-how and previous experience in Gaza. The FAO will engage Civil Society Organizations (CSOs) according to its set of rules and regulations to implement some of the tasks within 2.2, 3.2 and 3.3. # Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (GCF) Funding Proposal | Page 27 of 80

### Partnership Umbrella and Tripartite Agreement

The project will be implemented under a partnership umbrella and sign a tripartite project agreement between AFD, MoA (representing Palestine), and FAO for the joint implementation of the aforementioned activities.

FAO's coordination office for the West Bank and Gaza Strip programme is operating since 2002 and implements its programme through a team of 45 employees (35 nationals and 10 internationals) in 3 offices (Jerusalem, Ramallah, and Gaza). FAO has been working with the Palestinian government on water resources management for the Palestinian agriculture sector. FAO is central to stakeholder capacity development and for the facilitation of institutional arrangements.

In addition to its role as a technical partner, FAO also plays an important role in policy dialogue in close partnership with the Ministry of Agriculture. FAO recently supported the development of the Revised National Standards for the Use of Reclaimed Water/Waste for Irrigation, which defines the required qualities for different types of reclaimed water and sets out the criteria for its use.

FAO works closely with other international organizations such as UNDP, WFP, and GIZ, who share similar climate and socio-economic conditions. FAO is uniquely positioned to address the enabling conditions for effective implementation of the project in Palestine by providing a comprehensive approach that takes into account legal, regulatory, socio-cultural, economic, environmental and technical factors into consideration.

FAO in Jordan works also closely with FAO Head Quarters in Rome and FAO Regional Office (for the Middle East) in Cairo, which has been supporting projects to TWRI and water management in the MENA region.

In conclusion, all inputs will be implemented by the PWA, with the exception of 2.2.1, 2.2.3, 3.1.1, 3.2 and 3.3, implemented by MoA-FAO.

## C.6 Regulation, Taxation and Insurance (If Applicable)

Construction permits: They are usually delivered by Municipalities, but Government Institutions are exempt from this procedure.

Environmental permit: EQA has approved the updated version of the EQUA; the updated version of the RAP will be also submitted to the EQA.

Land titles: The project land titles are registered in PWA benefit as government land that was an acquisition of endowment land registered under the Waqf Ministry since 2013 (reference: FAO Council of ministers Decree #4/28/11 for the year 2013).

Taxation: All agreements signed with the PA are exempt from taxes and duties; the funding will get the required exemptions during the implementation as per the PA Tax Regulations.

Insurance: Following the PA procurement law and the signed funds financing agreements, Contractors are always requested to furnish adequate insurance policies within their contracts for the duration of the implementation period.


## C.7 Institutional / Implementation Arrangements

### Contracting Authority

AFD will sign a sub-contract agreement with the Ministry of Finance and Planning in Ramallah (representing the Palestinian government). This agreement and the related project documents will specify the tasks dedicated to the PWA and to the Ministry of Agriculture. The project will be implemented by two Executing Entities (EE): the PWA and FAO.


---
**Page 33**
---

For the implementation of the project, AfD will also enter into a Tripartite Project Agreements with, respectively, PWA and MoF and the FAO and MoA. For the avoidance of doubt, the tripartite project agreements will also be considered Subsidiary Agreements.

Accordingly, there will be a total of three Subsidiary Agreements for the implementation of the project.

AFD, as the AE, will be responsible for the project implementation and activities carried out by EEs.

Project implementation responsibility

The Ministry of Finance and Planning will sign an on-granting agreement with the PWA (the PWA is an independent public body; therefore, an on-granting agreement is required). The PWA will be the lead Executing Entity for the project and will be in charge of the overall coordination of the activities and of the "hard" components of the project.

The execution of the tripartite project agreement between AfD, MoPF (representing Palestine) and PWA will be a condition precedent to the first disbursement under the AfD-Palestine Subsidiary Agreement.

The Ministry of Agriculture according to the grant agreement will be responsible as Executing Entity for part of the project implementation, for activities related to on-farm development.

MoPF will not channel funds. It is the legal representative of Palestine for the signing of financial agreements with AfD in this project and will act as the implementing partner for Public Sector entities designated by Palestine and AfD to execute the project; in this case PWA. Disbursements are directly made to PWA (EE 1) or MoA-FAO (EE 2) or to Contractors.

Main stakeholders

The PWA - Executing Entity will be directly responsible of the project implementation apart from on-farm activities.

The Ministry of Agriculture - Executing Entity will be responsible for the on-farm components and connection points. The EAO will be the MoA's implementing partner for on-farm activities and support the civil society organizations. The FAO will sign an Implementing Partnership Agreement with the MoA, detailing the technical and financial characteristics of the project to be implemented by the MoA. The MoA will provide the necessary resources as well as specific knowledge and know-how of this organization in the context of Palestine and Gaza justifies that this H2H is a good fit for the MoA.

MoA and FAO have a sister relationship because Palestine's MoA is a FAO Member. The project will activate this pre-existing and non-commercial institutional relationship through a specific FAO-Country MoA Agreement directed by the FAO Country Representative. This agreement will be signed in parallel with the tripartite MoA agreement, which will be the tripartite project agreement between AfD, MoP (representing Palestine) and FAO. The MoA will specify the scope of work to be implemented by the FAO and will provide for "direct payment" modalities, which will be subject to financial sanction approval on a case basis based on timely approval of the workplan and implementation progress.

## Project Implementation

### Activities under the PWA responsibility

A project management unit (PMU) within the PWA will be responsible for all tendering activities for capital investments, including the procurement of international technical assistance and for the financial management of the project. All tendering activities will follow AFD procurement guidelines and will be subject to prior review by AFD at each stage of the procurement process: tender documents review; technical evaluation; financial evaluation; contract award. The PMU will also manage all contracts with contractors, including the preparation of necessary technical department and disbursement officer. All contracts for goods and works will be managed under “direct payment” procedure (the standard procedure described in AFD’s financing agreement), whereby the PWA is the paying agency on the basis of invoices from the contractors reviewed and transmitted by the PWA/PMU to AFD Jerusalem Office.

The PMU will include a Gender Action Plan Coordinator, with previous experience in coordinating Gender Studies, based in Jordan.

Further, the PWA will be responsible for the Groundwater Quality Monitoring Plan, in liaison with the MoA and EQA, and for the setting up of a communications protocol to inform WUs and users about possible water quality hazards. The PMU will also manage the implementation of the project’s monitoring plan, which includes actions ranging from restriction of use for agriculture to the temporary shutdown of the transfer of water to users and, if necessary, the immediate closure of wells in order to prevent contamination of groundwater by hazardous contaminants reaching acceptable levels. If case infiltration basins are full or “unavailable” (which is unlikely thanks to the rehabilitation of 7 additional basins through this project), an emergency discharge protocol will be triggered.

The PWA/PMU will also be assisted in its project management tasks by an international Technical Assistance.

### Activities located outside the mandate of the PWA

For activities related to on-farm activities, the Ministry of Agriculture of the Palestinian Authority will take over the implementation. The Ministry will seek the assistance of the FAO as its Implementation Partner for Outputs 2.2., 3.2 and 3.3. The MoA will also provide technical support in the capacity building of Local Community Social Organizations (CSOs) according to its sets of rules and regulations to implement parts of the tasks outlined in 2.1, 2.2 and 3.3.

## Land Rights

Project activities are expected to require minimal land acquisition for the construction of wells, pumping stations, effluent reuse pipelines and storage tanks. The PWA (EE) will adopt a comprehensive avoidance mechanism through the following procedures:

- **Construction of wells on State owned lands:** As such, the first phase of the project caused no impacts pertaining to land acquisition.

**Page 35**

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION (GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROGRAM) [PAGE 30 OF 80]

## Governance of the project

A Steering Committee will be established to ensure appropriate coordination between stakeholders of the project (PWA (the Chair), EQA, PENSA, Ministry of Finance and Planning, MoPP, Coastal Municipalities Water Utility/City Councils). The PWA is in a leading decision-making role and its guidance on decisions sharing: the Committee will be convened twice a year. AFD will be an external observer in this Committee.

The Steering Committee will follow the implementation of the project through the indicators and the logical framework.

A Technical Committee, limited to Implementing agencies (PWA (the Chair), MoS/FAO, WSRC), shall also be created for every meeting on operational decision making. It will report to the Steering Committee.

The secretariat of the two committees will be held by the PMU.

## AFD supervision of the project

AFD will provide no objections:

- Prior to the signing of the on-granting agreement with the PWA and the project agreement with FAO;
- On semi-annual technical and financial execution plans;
- On any other matter related to the project in accordance with its own procedures.

Bi-annual supervisions will take place, including the mobilization of expertise. A gender expert - AF D-staff is assigned for the implementation of the Gender Action Plan.

## Monitoring and evaluation

The PWA/PMU will be in charge of centralizing and managing the monitoring system of the project. In particular, the PMU will be responsible for organizing the Mid-term review, as well as the end-line final evaluation of the project.

AFD will tender and contract the consultancy services for the ex-post evaluation of the project, after full completion of the activities (usually one year after project completion).

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 37 OF 90

### D.X.Value Added for GCF Involvement

The GCF has been sought at the end line of a relatively long and costly investment starting with the creation of the NGEST WWTP and that will end with the proposed project. Conventional development banks have pushed to the limit the amount of funds dedicated to this endeavor as the World Bank has delivered its final contribution in 2016 for the NGEST WWTP, which was completed at a cost of $35 million. The GCF, however, in 2018, dedicate more than the amount of its country contribution to this project, usually capped at 10 MEUR/year.

This last push from conventional donors to this endeavor cuts short of converting a classical “water and sanitation” project into an integrated water resources management (IWRM) project. The GCF has been able to finance directly incremental costs related to closing the water cycle in order to make use of every drop of the 13 Mgal/year produced by the WWTP, composing effecting climate change on agricultural production, access to domestic water and livelihoods in Gaza.

In this rationale, the incremental costs necessary for the completion of each step of the water cycle (infiltration, low-carbon recovery, storage and distribution, efficient irrigation infrastructure) are considered eligible to the GCF contribution. The NGEST WWTP has been completed with a cost of 35 MEUR, which is the maximum budget per project through the project. Nevertheless, GCF funding has been concentrated on output 1, dedicated to generating water for domestic use and irrigation in Gaza. This output has been financed at a cost of 70% of its total budget, i.e., 9.5 MEUR/year. The incremental costs related to closing the water cycle are estimated at 3.5 MEUR/year.

The GCF contribution to this project is thus 13 MEUR/year, an exceptional effort has been made to bring this contribution to 13 MEUR in order to make it eligible for co-financing (of this project), singling out and justifying the very incremental nature of the GCF contribution to the project.

This low-carbon (i.e. with mitigation co-benefit) adaptation climate change element is the end-of-first milestone of the NGEST WWTP, which has been completed in 2016. The NGEST WWTP has been designed and implemented solving a water scarcity problem. As such, the AE considers that the climate value for money for GCF funds should be evaluated in light of the financial effort made by conventional donors from the start of the overall investment, i.e., the cost of the NGEST WWTP (35 MEUR) plus the incremental costs related to closing the water cycle (3.5 MEUR/year), through this investment has been delivered and commissioned prior to the present project and is now completed.

Therefore, the value for money of GCF funds sought to co-finance this project is calculated as follows:

| Water deficit related | Cost (MEUR) | GCF | GCF cofinancing ratio of water deficit related investments |
|---|---|---|---|
| WWTP | 43 | 0.0 | - |
| Recovery | 13.6 | 7.2 | 53.9% |
| Irrigation | 8.4 | 4.4 | 52.4% |
| Capacity building water sector | 1.85 | 0.75 | 40.5% |
| Total | 73.55 | 13.55 | 29.5% |

### CLIMATE VALUE FOR MONEY

Climate vulnerability / Climate financing Ratio = **131.7%**

It is apparent that the 22.5 % of co-financing of GCF to the global NGEST program is of the same magnitude as “the climate change contribution” of 30% to the growing vulnerability of the Gaza population due to increasing water scarcity (see C2 and Annex 3), bringing GCF’s climate value for money at roughly 110%.

4 206 beneficiaries (farm owners, permanent and seasonal labor) will benefit from improved access to water, increasing their resilience to Climate Change over the lifespan of the hydraulic infrastructures. Each farmer requires an average of 15 m3/year for irrigation according to the Palestinian Central Bureau of Statistics which leads to 23 553 people with enhanced livelihoods.

The 13 Mm³ infiltrated and recovered for agriculture will reduce their measures to combat risks by alleviating the pressure on the aquifer. The additional water resources will be used to cover the domestic needs of the population, it is expected that this additional resource will "free" water otherwise used by agriculture (in the baseline scenario) that will immediately be used to cover the domestic needs of the growing population in Gaza Strip. This will allow for a significant increase in the number of people who have access to safe drinking water which is extremely low; the project will allow covering the domestic needs of 200 000 people over the lifetime of the infrastructure.

The total impact of the project is therefore of **23 553 people**.

**E2. Paradigm Shift Potential**

The degree to which the proposed activity can catalyze impact beyond a one-off project/programme investment.

### E2.1. Potential for scaling up and replication (Provide a numerical multiple and supporting rationale)

In the context of Palestine, including Gaza, it is a humanitarian and development imperative every drop of water be recycled in order to compensate the effects of climate change as well as scarcity and increase the resilience and adaptation capacity of the population.

The project is to be implemented based on the experience in Palestine and will serve as a model to multiply the experiences throughout its territory. At design stage, as an adaptive response, all new sanitation and WWTP projects will include a "REUSE" component for agriculture and associated activities related to on-farm diversification (e.g., date palm groves). This is also expected in other projects of the same scale (e.g., PV schemes). Tested for the first time at this scale, they will also be systematically included in future projects.

The results of the mid-term evaluation of this project will be strategic and timely inputs to ongoing or future REUSE projects in Palestine. The project will also provide a model for the reuse of treated wastewater (TWW) water for the Palestinian agriculture in the Jordan Valley); at the Jericho WWTP to irrigate palm tree groves for date production; at the Nablus WWTP where the German cooperation is supporting a pilot of direct reuse of TWW for agriculture (40 ha). It is also expected from this project to be potentially duplicated to other projects in the region.


---
**Page 47**
---

# E.2.2 Potential for knowledge and learning

The core component of the project, as the first real scale REUSE by aquifer recharge operation in Palestine, is both huge and highly strategic in order to foster and strengthen the adaptive capacity to climate change of Palestine and its population.

In fact, at present, Palestine has no other short-term option on the table to start reversing its water deficit than the reuse of TWW. Nevertheless, up-scaling this effort calls for the mastering of the several dimensions of reuse of TWW:

- Technological: what is the best solution in terms of cost and quality to deliver the best service to end-users?
- Economic, Social and environmental: how do we guarantee quality and stability of the water delivered (also in terms of quantity) over time? How can we ensure its sustainability?

Sustainability: how do we create and support functional and legitimate users' institutions that take over all or part of the O&M of the infrastructure delivered by the public sector?

These questions are not only relevant for the project itself, but also for the future. They will need to be documented and analyzed through the present project, its external evaluations and by the mobilization of funds earmarked for the sustainability of the project (see section 2.3.1). The creation of a platform (e.g., Water Innovation Lab), a platform created by AFD and bringing together professionals and researchers in water and irrigation, could be mobilized and could cofinance this work in the amount of 150 000 EUR in addition to the financing of the project.

This will allow for the creation of a knowledge base that can be used as an input for other projects in the region.


# E.2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

As mentioned above, NGEST is but the first step of a relatively long path to full development of REUSE capacity in Palestine. The successful implementation and sustainability of the NGEST project will pave the way to similar projects in other parts of the territories.

In particular, the following aspects of the project will be particularly enabling for the development in the future:

- The creation of an institutional framework that includes a specific funding mechanism which is paramount to the success of any REUSE project, will have to be the standards of all future projects;
- The involvement of the Water Sector Regulatory Council as an external oversight body, will be essential to the sustainability and long-term success of the project in terms of both technical and financial aspects;
- The stronger inclusion of on-farm activities, a key aspect of the sustainability of reuse of TWW in agriculture, which is currently the role of MoA and FAO will set a precedent to a more holistic approach to the management of the water cycle;
- The creation of the WUA and the importance of women therein, which will lead to a more equitable and gender-balance access and distribution of water.

### E.2.4 Contribution to regulatory frameworks and policies

The successful delivery of water is a complex multi-stakeholder endeavor, and quality control at each level of the water cycle is paramount to the success of any such project. The Water Sector Regulatory Council serves exactly that purpose and, in the context of Palestine, its strengthening and capacity building is dependent on direct funding.

The main responsibilities of the WSRC as per the water law can be detailed as follows:

- Approval of water tariffs, costs of supply networks and other services required for the delivery of water and wastewater.
- Regular monitoring and enforcement of these tariffs to ensure compliance with the policy adopted by the Palestinian Water Authority.
- The issuance of licenses to Regional Water Utilities or any operator that establishes or manages the operation, production, distribution, collection, or treatment of water or the collection and treatment of wastewater, and the levying of license fees.
- Monitoring and inspection of compliance with the terms, requirements and indicators stipulated in licenses and permits.
- The development of performance incentives programs for water service providers.
- Monitoring operation processes related to the production, transport, and distribution of water and operational processes of wastewater management.
- Setting quality assurance standards for the provision of technical and administrative services by Service Providers.
- Monitoring the compliance of the National Water Company and Service Providers with the adopted standards for the provision of water and sanitation services.
- The establishment of a database for technical, financial and statistical information and the publication of this information periodically.

Addressing complaints of consumers against Service Providers. 

The project will integrate the WSC into the steering and the technical committees in order to allow it to exercise its oversight role over the regulatory framework, the development of the project and stakeholder mobilization in the context of a water users association.

# E.3 Sustainable Development Potential

## E.3.1 Environmental, social and economic co-benefits, including gender-sensitive development impact

By embracing several developmental dimensions in the same project, the proposal has a strong potential when it comes to sustainable development.

The aim of this report is going into the details of the different dimensions of sustainable development, to highlight the following SDGs, which are targeted by this project: namely SDG 1 (no poverty), SDG 2 (zero hunger), SDG 3 (good health and well-being), SDG 6 (clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy), SDG 8 (decent work and economic growth), SDG 9 (industry, innovation and infrastructure), SDG 10 (reduced inequalities), SDG 13 (climate action).

## Environment

Since 2009, about 30 million cubic meters of untreated water have been infiltrated in the aquifer at the location of NGEST; this has created a pollution plume which is moving towards the West and should reach the proposed location of the new power plant in 2015. The project will contribute to reducing the pollution plume, improving the quality of the water and the health of the population of Gaza City (See Baseline Study On Water Quality &Public Health In The Gaza Strip, 2013, Annex A). The activation of the water cycle downstream from the NGEST WWTP by installing a PV system will contribute to reducing the pollution plume. This will also contribute to improving the quality and prevent the occurrence of a health crisis in Gaza City: (i) infiltration of treated waste water will dilute the pollution plume, (ii) the installation of a PV system will reduce the pressure on the aquifer and thus the pollution caused by pumping (see image below) will reverse the progression of the pollution plume towards the surrounding areas.

Further infiltration of water in the aquifer after agricultural use will contribute to depolluting the water underground and sustaining it for the benefit of the population. Reduction in Greenhouse Gas (GHG) through the installation of a 1 ha PV system is also an environmental co-benefit.

## Socioeconomic co-benefits of the project are mainly twofold:

(i) In a context where every day more farmers are abandoning their land, the rehabilitation of agriculture on all or some non-irrigated lands will free the agricultural quantity of effort and resources in the context of a fast growing population, which in the baseline scenario would take place any further depolluting the aquifer and generating health hazards due to bad quality of water. It is estimated that, thanks to the project, 200,000 people
