### Gender empowerment:
Key gender issues experienced by women in the project area that were revealed by the gender analysis are:

- adverse social norms;
- discrimination and lack of legal protection;
- the failure to recognize, reduce and redistribute unpaid household work and care; and
- a lack of access to financial and property assets.

E.4. Needs of the Recipient

Vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population

E.4.1. Vulnerability of the Gaza Strip population

Conflict and climate vulnerability in Gaza has been extensively studied by Michael MASON from the London School of Economics, who develops a very useful analysis of the two coexisting and combining factors.

In conflict-affected areas, the impact of climate change is likely to be increased by their exposure to violence and other coercive practices. Stakeholder assessments of climate vulnerability, as reported here for the Gaza Strip, can capture the qualitative experience of harm caused by conflict-related practices as these relate to climate change. The following figure, adapted from the work of M. MASON et al., in Gaza is also included in Palestine-UNDP Adaptation on Climate Change Action Plan (UNDP 2016a, p.16).

The prevalence in Gaza of short-term "enforced coping" prevents the development of long-term adaptive capacity. Climate vulnerability assessments in [post]conflict environments should acknowledge the methodological and political-policy challenges associated with such an approach.

Conflict and social vulnerability to climate change: Lessons from Gaza, Climate and Development, 3/4, 285-297)

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## E4.2 Financial, economic, social and institutional needs

### Economic and social development level of the country and the affected population

Despite its potential, the Palestinian economy is currently heavily distorted and failing to generate the jobs and incomes needed to improve living standards. Restrictions on trade and access to resources, along with a decade long blockade by Israel, have severely impacted economic activity in Gaza. The agricultural sector has shrunk significantly in the last twenty-five years, while agriculture is only one third its previous size. The economy is import dependent with imports over three times the size of exports and a trade deficit close to 40 percent of GDP (one of the highest in the world). The economic structure is dominated by low productivity services which are heavily reliant on foreign aid and relatively unproductive activities that generate insufficient employment. As a result, growth, which has mainly been driven by remittances from Palestinians working abroad, has been slow and uneven. In 2016, GDP per capita reached levels, in real Gross Domestic Product (GDP) slowed to two percent on average between 2013 and 2016, and dropped to a mere 0.7 percent in early 2017. Unemployment remains close to 30 percent on average, with youth unemployment reaching over 50 percent.

### Historical context

Historically, the agriculture sector has been the largest economic sector in the Gaza Strip and still plays an important role in the economy. This role, however, has declined over time in an unsustained context. The contribution of the agricultural sector to GDP continued declining after the Oslo agreement, reaching 10% in the 1990s. Intensification of restrictions on movement of people and goods affected the marketing and export of agricultural products. This caused further decline in the contribution of agriculture to GDP which reached only 5% by 2004. The sector contributed about 23% and constituting 14% of the employment. In 2005, Israel withdrew occupation forces and removed settlements from the Gaza Strip. However, the blockade continued and the agricultural sector in Gaza was severely affected. Since then, the West Bank (WB) and Gaza Strip. Between 2005 and 2015, after the establishment of the blockade, the contribution of the agricultural sector to GDP remained between 5% and 8.5%, reaching 5.7% in 2015. The latest Palestinian Central Bureau of Statistics (PCBS) reported that agriculture accounted for only 3.4 percent of total workers in the Gaza Strip work in agriculture and fishing, and the agriculture sector recorded the lowest overall productivity.

### E.5.1 Existence of a national climate strategy and coherence with existing plans and policies, including NAPs, NAPAs and NAPS

On the 17th of March 2016, Palestine officially became the Tenth party to the UNFCCC. It also ratified the Paris Agreement on the 22nd of April 2016 and was one of the first countries to do so. Few months after joining the UNFCCC Palestine was able to submit its Initial National Communication Report (INCR) and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for 2017-2022. The INCR and NAP were submitted in accordance with the guidelines of the UNFCCC, which reflects the very high priority given to adaptation to climate change by the Palestinian Authority.

The INCR, which was developed in accordance with the recommended and applicable guidelines, highlights the key outcomes on Greenhouse Gas (GHG) emission inventories, analysis of mitigation and adaptation potentials, making a vital contribution towards the achievement of the national climate strategy. The NAP is also an important tool to address the adverse effect of climate change.

The Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) was build upon the INCR and NAP and in line with the National Development Strategy. The NDC formulated the National Policy Agenda (2017-2022), the highest policy paper which clearly indicates that climate change and effective response to it as a priority. Palestine has developed its strategic plan for 2016-2025, which is in line with the NDCs and NAP and takes climate change into consideration. In this document could be found Water Sector Strategy, Agriculture Sector Strategy and Environment Cross Section Strategy which include goals linked to climate change mitigation and/or adaptation.

Palestine together with AE have prepared this project based on key adaptation actions prioritised in the NAP: "Enhance the use of additional and alternative water resources for nondomestic purposes". The activities of this project are aligned with the national priorities as well as the sectoral strategies of the 12 priority sectors including: water, wastewater, health and others. It clearly improves water security by creating new and additional resources to the country which is greatly suffering from lack of water resources and allows enhanced agricultural production.

The gender responsive technology road map formulation, which will identify relevant mitigation and adaptation technologies to achieve the Palestine's climate change action plans as defined in its NDC, NAP and NDC is currently ongoing. Although the development of the technology road map is still at an early stage, stakeholders have already identified a number of priority areas for further investigation and analysis. These include technologies related to efficient irrigation, water harvesting, climate smart agriculture, water resource monitoring technologies, and the treatment and reuse of wastewater. The roadmap will further explore |

The NAP was developed in accordance with the IPCC's 5th Assessment Report definitions. Vulnerabilities were prioritized for the NAP in relation to climate sensitivities and adaptive capacities. Adaptation options were then identified that address each of the highly vulnerable sectors (e.g., agriculture, water resources, coastal zones, energy, health, etc.). The criteria used are cost-effectiveness, efficiency, timing/urgency, social acceptance, technology, knowledge/skills, costs, and co-benefits for adaptation and mitigation). The resultant NAP was endorsed by all six relevant ministries.

A recent review of the priority of NDC conditional actions with stakeholders across from the Agriculture sector in relation to climate change vulnerability, adaptive capacity, capacity available and technology available was performed. This process has reconfirmed the importance of the NDC actions, in particular the ones that are the basis of this project.

### E.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

Please describe experience and track record of the accredited entity and executing entities with respect to the activities that they will be implementing in the proposed programme/project.

## AFD and Climate Change

AFD was one of the first international donors to integrate the fight against climate change into its practices. This approach has been formalized since 2015, when it adopted a strategy for climate action, which is intrinsically linked to the trajectory and development policies of countries, in terms of opportunities and threats, as well as the involvement of economic, institutional and civil society actors. AFD’s first climate strategy dates from 2003.

Since then and in line with its national positioning and its involvement in ecological and climate diplomacy, France has been a strong proponent of the Paris Agreement. In this context, the French government progressively adjusted its intervention strategy and committed more than EUR 29 billion between 2005 and 2017 to projects and programmes designed to provide climate-to-benefit.

AFD’s approach is based on three pillars, each a marker of its identity, with three main aspects: (i) systematically measuring the carbon footprint of the operations financed (carbon balance) and assessing their potential benefits in terms of reduction or compensation for greenhouse gas emissions; (ii) selection of projects that contribute to projects with climate co-benefits; and (iii) selectivity of projects in terms of impact on climate, taking into account the level of development of the countries concerned. This strategy has profoundly transformed AFD’s portfolio and underpinned its positioning as a reference for climate action at international financial institutions.

This strong position has contributed to the AFD Group's significant visibility on climate issues at both national and international levels. It has also enabled it to play an active role in the development of the Paris Agreement, notably by AFD was particularly involved in issues of accounting and deployment of climate finance, metrics in terms of impact, and a new approach to financing climate change.

The new international momentum, transcribed in the SDGs and the Paris Agreement, requires AFD to support fundamental and rapid changes in the development models of countries and economic actors even more ambitiously. Moreover, the implementation of the Paris Agreement is not only an issue for France but also for its partners. The implementation of the Paris Agreement not only in France but also at the international level. This provides AFD with a new mandate to implement the Paris Agreement on behalf of its partners. To do so, it must continue to fulfill its mandate to implement the Paris Agreement across its entire portfolio. AFD must therefore continue the transformation of its methods and instruments, launched within the framework of its previous climate strategies.

Three strategic challenges have been identified concerning the role and place of AFD in climate action in the years to come: (i) ensuring that all projects financed by AFD are consistent with the Paris Agreement and contribute to climate-resilient development and related public policies; maximizing the impact of its actions in this regard, notably in terms of leverage effects and strengthening AFD’s role as a platform for France's international financial commitment; and (iii) reinforcing its positioning as a reference on climate development and among international finance institutions.

The new AFD Climate and Development Strategy for 2017-2022 thus proposes three main objectives: (i) ensure that all projects financed by AFD are consistent with the Paris Agreement, (ii) increase the impact of climate finance, (iii) contribute to reducing financing risks and investment flows, and (iv) co-build solutions and bring influence to bear on standards.

In terms of parameters and scope, the first commitment entails a fundamental evolution of the AFD Group’s approach to climate action. This involves progressing from an approach mainly based on an assessment of projects’ climate benefits towards an approach that includes seeking compliance of all interventions with low-carbon and climate-resilient development pathways.

The AFD Group operates in the Palestinian Territories since 1998 and has financed, until 2017, about fifty projects, representing a total of more than €365 million.

AFD operates and supports national strategies in various sectors (Water and Sanitation, local development, private sector, environment, etc.) through three main approaches:

- Strengthening public institutions and service providers by supporting the development through the implementation of new services;
- Maintain and improve the living conditions of the Palestinian population;
- Support economic actors in the private sector;
- Stimulate innovation;
- Development of rural territories including transportation and solid waste management, etc.;
- Mitigation and adaptation to the impacts of climate change;
- Promoting renewable energy and energy efficiency.

The water and sanitation sector has been AFD's priority intervention sector, with 13 water and sanitation investment programs financed in the Palestinian Territories for a total amount of nearly €106 million (excluding land acquisition costs). These projects have enabled 250,000 people to access drinking water and/or to benefit from improved drinking water services. Another project has enabled 800,000 people to gain access to drinking water and/or to benefit from improvement in drinking water services.

The motivations of interdiction of AFD in the sector have evolved from projects focusing on production and transfer of drinking water to broader sectoral interventions:

- Supporting the sector reform: establishment of joint service models to rationalize the service delivery, supporting the Palestinian Water Authority (PWA) in 2014 that led to the creation of the Water Sector Regulatory Council (WSRC), supporting the establishment of a National Water Company to manage the conventional and un-conventional water resources. Currently AFD's new intervention, the Nexus North project, committed in 2017 to support the PWA in its reform by piloting the distribution model and promoting public-private partnerships by focusing on their empowerment.
- The construction of wastewater treatment plants: the construction of a wastewater treatment plant in the northern Gaza Strip and Hebron, construction of a wastewater treatment plant in Misliya. A new project is under study by AFD for the construction of a sewage treatment plant in Bethlehem.
- The reuse of treated water: this objective has always been an integral part of investments programs to maximize the benefit from water resources in a water scarcity environment.

The three above-mentioned themes, targeted by AFD, are at the heart of the Palestinian Authority's sectoral priorities:

- Effective implementation of the water sector reform initiated in 2014.
- The treatment of the 100% of the wastewater flowing from the Palestinian Territories to Israel today, with the objective of reducing the volume of treated wastewater arriving on their territory and charge this treatment to Palestinians) that reached 35M3/day.
- Achievement of 100% REUSE: reuse of all treated wastewater for agricultural purposes. The agriculture sector is currently consuming around 97% of demand from water.

The following table contemplates, in Outputs 4 and 5, the economic and financial profitability acquired through an improved access to water showing, in an ideal scenario, that improved access to water and improving crop yields will lead to a significant increase of the Market Return of Rates (MRR), making us believe that the project may create an island of prosperity in its area of intervention.

The reality of water deficit in Palestine and its particular acuteness in Gaza is such that every cubic meter added to the water balance has immediate and multiple knock-on effects on every dimension of vulnerability and survival of the population. Over the lifespan of the infrastructure, i.e., 30 years, the AE is confident that in the context of extreme vulnerability of the population and the accelerating effects of climate change on an already dire scenario, the economic rate of return of a non-upgraded system is extremely high. Conversely, not upgrading the system will reduce recovery power by 95%, which means that the project will have no effect on reducing the probability of rupture and failure to zero.

Reasoning in terms of water flow rather than as cash-flow – the actualization of which over time is a mere theoretical calculation – the project will allow the aquifer to sustain the livelihoods and associated economic activity of an additional 200,000 people at the current level of access to water over the lifetime of the financed infrastructure. In this way, the population will be able to withstand the effects of demographic growth; will find itself, thus being equal, both with no access to water or depleting the last resources for the following generations. Without the GCF support, we remain in the baseline scenario.

### E.6.4 Application of best practices

Please explain how best available technologies and practices are considered and applied. If applicable, specify the innovations/modifications/adaptations that are made based on industry best practices.

The following text describes the application of best practices in the context of the role of the aquifer as storage and buffer) and Managed Aquifer Recharge (MAR). It is extensively studied and described by the scientific community (for example: Multi-Objective Optimization of Managed Aquifer Recharge, Ayubul Fathullah et al., April 2019) and can be found in the following document: https://www.mau.ac.uk/led/files/groundwater-and-climate-change-2015/file/104386.pdf, in particular in the section on "Groundwater Recharge".

"Jordan like many other semi-arid countries in the world is highly dependent on groundwater resources and face increasing demand and decreasing supply leading to groundwater level declines and potentially salinization of groundwater. Managed Aquifer Recharge (MAR) has been defined as the intentional recharge of groundwater for recovery or environmental purposes and includes the monitoring of recharge water quality and resulting impacts. It has been applied successfully internationally and its importance is increasingly recognized in Jordan, especially with regard to climate change" (https://www.hkp.bund.de/EN/Themen/Wasser/Projekte/jbgeschlossen/TZ/Jordanien/mar_fb_en.html).

The Gaza population presents an extremely high level of vulnerability and of uncertainty with regard to the future. The situation is characterized by a very high degree of poverty, unemployment, and social instability. The emergency on the water resources, the emergency of the situation, the humanitarian rationale of adaptation to climate change and the urgency of the situation in Gaza area, all these factors make it necessary to provide a financial structure calls for the highest level of concessionality for the delivery of infrastructure, dedicated to recharging the aquifer, which is considered a good public. ## F.2 Technical Evaluation

Water banking or MAR is a technological approach to artificial aquifer recharge in contexts where water scarcity calls for the development of alternative sources of water. In case of Gaza recycling of treated waste water is one of only two short/medium term solutions to alleviate pressure on the aquifer and maintain minimal access to agriculture, while also providing an important co-benefit in terms of sanitation. The other option is desalination (representing much more costly, highly sensitive and complex investments). The sandiness of the soil in Gaza makes it a particularly adapted location to perform MAR due to the high infiltration capacity of such soil.

Further, the MAR scheme proposed in this project includes an off-grid solar energy production field and a net metering system that will allow for the export of excess electricity generated on the local grid. This is a response to the third element of the vulnerability nexus in Gaza: energy insecurity.

Finally, the proposed approach, through MAR in Gaza, will generate an important climate co-benefit, as well as in environmental terms, through the use of the WWA and NOGAS as it is a clean “washing machine” depolluting the aquifer and preventing a health crisis in the downstream Gaza PFO.

## Environmental Methodology

### Hydro Model Update and for Groundwater Analyses Verification and Modeling

The most updated data provided by the client, up to year 2017, was used to update the model, accounting for the following:

- The actual infiltrated partially treated wastewater quantities and rates from 2012 to 2017 (15,000 - 20,000 m³/day).
- The actual capacities and numbers of the recovery wells.
- The actual design of the first stage of the recovery wells (14 wells) that were constructed by the end of year 2017.
- The updated time schedule for the operation of the treatment plant and the two stages of the recovery wells.

The assessment of the impacts on groundwater considered the abstraction rates of the recovery wells, the possible changes in the water table levels, and different scenarios for project implementation. Two scenarios are considered in the current impact assessment:

1. Without the implementation of recovery scheme.
2. With the implementation of recovery scheme: 7 recovery wells will be implemented on two stages; 14 wells that already constructed and to be operated by the end of 2019 and 15 wells to be operated by the end of 2021.

Both scenarios take into account the operation of the WWTP by the beginning of 2018. It is important to note that partially treated wastewater continued to be infiltrated until the beginning of 2018. Since the beginning of 2018, only treated wastewater is infiltrated and will reach its full capacity of 35,000 m³/day of treated wastewater by June 2018.

## Important Environmental Outcomes and Considerations

- No significant changes have occurred in any of the physical or biological environment of the project areas since the beginning of the monitoring.
- The recent monitoring results reveal nitrate concentrations ranging from 20mg/l to 70 mg/l in 2017, both in the monitored wells and in the groundwater samples collected at the end of year 2017. These numbers far exceed the WHO standards that indicate a maximum value of 55 mg/l for nitrate, and other heavy metals are also present.
- Pathogenic bacteria was also found in the groundwater in monitoring wells in close proximity to the infiltration basin, since partially treated sewage has been infiltrating the aquifer for 9 years.

Heavy metal concentrations were analyzed from all monitored wells. The results show that these values exceed the Palestinian standard values for irrigation. While total iron concentration is within the Palestinian standard value of 10 mg/l, this only the case in very few monitoring wells and would therefore not limit the use in irrigation as a general


---
**Page 64**
---

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 59 OF 80

**E**

rule: It is only recommended that these elements are continuously monitored to avoid water use where limits are exceeded.

5. All other major water parameters monitored in first stage recovery wells such as Acidity (pH), E.C., T.B.S., T.A., R.O.D., S.O4, and K have been found to be compliant with Palestinian standards.
6. Water quality is expected to improve significantly after the operation of the recovery wells due to internationally and locally acceptable irrigation practices.

As a result of the improved water quality, higher nitrogen levels may be beneficial during early growth stages but may cause yield losses during the latter flowering and fruiting stages. High nitrogen water can be used as a fertilizer early in the season. However, as the nitrogen needs of the crop increase with time, the use of high-nitrogen water should be reduced or substantially blended. Blending or changing supplies during the later more critical growth stages would be highly recommended to avoid yield losses and ensure that the crop is not over-fertilized by the irrigation water more effectively. Also, the rates of nitrogen fertilizer supplied to the crop should be reduced by an amount very nearly equal to that available from the water supply for crops irrigated with water containing high levels of nitrates. This will help in reducing the nitrate content of the water during the non-irrigation season.

According to simulation results, the situation is expected to be greatly improved after the operation of the recovery wells and nitrate levels will be reduced to internationally and locally acceptable irrigation standards.

7. The current average table elevation in the area around the basins is 2 m above mean sea level. After the operation of the first stage of recovery wells by the end of year 2019, about 20,000 m3/day of groundwater will be pumped from the aquifer and used for irrigation purposes. The water table elevation in this area would be reduced to a depth of 4-6 m below mean sea level after two years of operation of the first stage of recovery wells. In 2030, the model estimated that the water table elevation would be at about 15 m below mean sea level if the second stage of recovery wells is not implemented. While, in the same area, the water table elevation will be between 4 and 6 m below mean sea level if the second stage is implemented; as about 80,000 m3/day of groundwater will be pumped from the aquifer for irrigation purposes.

The vertical building up of the water table, which without recovery will have a negative impact on current crops.

## Social Consultation Methodology and Activities

The research team for this study has adopted multi-dimensional consultation activities that enable the marginalized, vulnerable, youth and women to gain information about the project. As well as gaining information about their views and concerns regarding the project during various implementation phases.

Following are the main consultation activities performed until June 2018 (additional engagement activities will take place until mid-July):

* Field visit to define various stakeholders during April 2018.
* Meeting with the Ministry of Water in April 2018 in order to develop an engagement plan that is locally tailored for the residential communities.
* Based on the identification of stakeholders, various questionnaires and guidelines were prepared in order to engage them. These include: i) Local Governmental municipalities, ii) the CBs, iv) health facility, v) Ministry of Endowment and Ministry of Agriculture, vi) the EQA

# G.1 Risk Assessment Summary

Please provide a summary of main risk factors. Detailed description of risk factors and mitigation measures can be elaborated in G.2.

## Main risk factors are related to:

- **Management of water quality for agriculture:** A monitoring system will be set up by the project and managed by PWA in coordination with EQA to perform day-to-day control of the quality of the water injected into the distribution network, as well as the maintenance and tuning of the monitoring system.
  
- **Energy needs of the Managed Aquifer Recharge scheme:** The project includes alternate sources of energy (other than the grid and generators) through solar panels and a bio-digester.

- **Acceptance of treated wastewater:** In general, social acceptance can be a problem, especially in Muslim countries. However, the experience with the NGEST WWTP strongly mitigates the social risk presented above. There will nevertheless be no major community opposition to this aspect and make sure there is no confusion and residual reluctance to using the water (it will also depend on the quality of the water delivered).

# G.2 Risk Factors & Mitigation Measures

Please describe financial, technical and operational, social and environmental and other risks that might prevent the project/programme objectives from being achieved. Also describe the proposed risk mitigation measures.

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The water in the aquifer is not depleted fast enough | Social and environmental | Medium (5-10% of project value) | Low |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The NGEST WWTP provides a high quality secondary treatment. Infiltration through 60 meters of sand completes the secondary treatment to a point that the water, when it enters the aquifer, is considered clean. It will therefore be impossible for the treated water to infiltrate into the groundwater and contaminate the drinking water and will therefore not be drinkable. Modeling of the aquifer and initial tests implemented by the PWA confirm it will be possible to use this water for irrigation purposes.

It is therefore crucial that the water cycle through the activation of the recovery wells is initiated as soon as possible, in order to stop the progression of the pollution plume and guarantee that uses of the underground water will not be affected in any way.


---
**Page 68**
---

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 63 OF 80)

| Selected Risk Factor 2 | Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lack of energy to run the system | Technical and operational | Medium (5-20% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The negotiation between Palestine and Israel for access to electricity of the Gaza Strip has been going on for years and is part of a political discussion.

At this stage Palestine and the local authorities in Gaza have secured an average of 16 hours per day of electricity from the grid for the NGEST WWTP, which is enough to run the plant. Nevertheless, in general, access to energy remains a major issue in Gaza.

In this context, the project has taken every possible step to mitigate the energy insecurity in Gaza by diversifying, as much as possible, the energy mix of the overall MAR scheme: energy from the grid; cogeneration using byproducts of the WWTP; and, in particular, climate-friendly off-grid solar energy production over 8.5 ha.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

### Description
Social acceptance of TWB

### Risk category
High (20% of project value)

### Level of impact
Low

### Mitigation Measure(s)
In theory the use of TWB in Muslim countries poses acceptance issues by the population, namely for religious reasons. In growing anddry situations, as in Tunisia for instance, these social barriers can be overcome with growing awareness by the population.

In the case of this project and, in general with MAR, the TWB is infiltrated in the aquifer, blended with underground water to be recovered at the end of the cycle. The blending of surface water with groundwater and cultural issues mentioned above can arise, and the water distributed to farmers is therefore quasi-conventional. Awareness raising on this issue will nevertheless be necessary due to the proximity of the recovery point to the village. It should be noted that the willingness of the population to cooperate with such will be crucial in establishing their trust with the provider. This is part of the FAO assignment which has deep know-how and experiences with MAR in Palestine on this topic.

### H.1. Logic Framework: Specify the logic framework in accordance with the GCF's Performance Measurement Framework under the Results Management Framework.

#### III.L. Paradigm Shift Objectives and Impacts at the Fund level[1]

**Paradigm shift objectives:** Mitigation objective: Reinforce the sustainability of the Water-Energy nexus for the NGEST system.
The project will show how energy solutions related to survival of a whole population context of Gaza, can be thought and designed along low emission and sustainable pathways, the coherency of which are an increased resilience to shocks and an increased efficiency with regard to water management costs.

Three co-benefits are expected from this project:
- Increased resilience of Gaza agriculture through the provision of improved water services for irrigation;
- Access to safe and improved access to domestic water of 200,000 people exposed to water scarcity;
- Depollution and preservation of the aquifer.

As a result of achieving these objectives, the process of pollution and overexploitation of the aquifer will be stopped before they reach an irreversible status.

# H2. Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

Besides the arrangements (e.g., semi-annual performance reports) laid out in AMA, please provide project/programme specific arrangements for monitoring and reporting on the key outcomes of the project.

Please indicate how the interim-term and final evaluations will be organized, including the timing. The project will have a special evaluation team to monitor, assess, analyze, and evaluate. Apart from the necessary technical evaluation tools, the PMU will indeed develop with the assistance of the Technical Assistance, the institutional framework of the project includes the Water Sector Regulatory Council as an external body responsible for monitoring and evaluating the project's achievements, as per the Palestinian Water Law requirements and standards.

Roles and responsibilities of the WSRC in the project need to be detailed in the inception report stage. Indeed, the referral of the water sector regulatory council is a legal requirement and more specifically, the independence of WSRC is unfortunately not yet formally agreed and approved.

In this context, the PMU will be responsible for coordinating and managing the monitoring system of the project. In particular, the PMU will be responsible for co-organizing the Mid-term review, as well as the end-line final evaluation of the project, in line with the incoming GCF guidelines.

AFD will tender and contract the consultancy services for the ex-post evaluation of the project, after full completion of the activities (usually one year after project completions).

Please provide methodologies for monitoring and reporting of the key outcomes of the project/programme. The MoA-FAO will be responsible for providing technical support to the PMU in this regard.

The MoA-FAO will report semi-annually as well on off-farm, agricultural activities. Independent data sources will be mobilized and used for reporting purposes.

For both PWA and FAO, an M&E specialist will be involved in gathering data in order to update indicators of key outcomes of the project. All inputs under each activity are designed in order to contribute the key outcomes of the project. At project completion, the indicator(s) it is reported on behalf of the project outcome will be updated after project completion. Periodic reporting will then present what has been completed on the path towards the outcomes...

Ramallah / Palestine, November 21, 2017

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by French Development Agency (AFD) regarding Water Banking and Adaptation of Agriculture to Climate Change in Northern Gaza

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project: Water Banking and Adaptation of Agriculture to Climate Change in Northern Gaza in Palestine as included in the funding proposal submitted to us by French Development Agency (AFD) on November 23, 2017.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Environment Quality Authority, the National Designated Authority of Palestine.

Pursuant to GCF decision no.8.6(10), the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our objection to this project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Palestine has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Palestine's national priorities, strategies and plans.
(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for accertaining no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Secretariat's assessment of FP119

Proposal name: Water Banking and Adaptation of Agriculture to Climate Change in Northern Gaza

Accredited entity: Agence Française de Développement (AFD)

Country/[ies]: State of Palestine

Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project has a strong climate rationale. Acute water scarcity in the State of Palestine is strongly linked with the climate vulnerability, and this is particularly true for rural areas and communities. | Access to energy in the project location could be limited. The photovoltaic system installed through the project will have a 25 per cent autonomy from the grid. In case of an emergency scheme; the rest will be secured through the grid. |
| The project demonstrates good value for money for GCF funding. The 30 per cent contribution to the total cost is estimated as growing vulnerability of the target population. The State of Palestine's water scarcity is estimated to have a climate value for money of approximately 130 per cent. | Social acceptance around treated wastewater could be low. The residence time of water in the aquifer is not known, which may heighten this risk. The creation of Water Users Associations (WUAs) will be essential to involve the most vulnerable farmers, including women, into project activities. |
| The proposed solution has been demonstrated regionally. Managed aquifer recharge is considered as a promising alternative providing direct transfer of treated wastewater to farmers and generating economic quality and environmental co-benefits. |  |

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in the annexure term sheet and addendum XVIII titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

The State of Palestine is characterized by high climate variability that impacts upon its already depleted water resources. It is estimated that the State of Palestine will experience a


---
**Page 90**
---

were drawn down to 211 million cubic metres per year by 2020 due to climate change and population growth. Furthermore, the coastal aquifer is the only freshwater resource in the area, serving the needs of the population and agricultural activities, but its water level is declining at a rapid rate, resulting in reduced yields from agriculture and increased salinity levels in groundwater. Inefficient irrigation practices and other inefficiencies in the area lead to the overuse of water and high evaporation, putting additional pressure on the coastal aquifer under the increasing effects of climate change. As this respect, the project aims at mitigating these impacts by adapting to climate change via water balance through adding new resources to the water balance in the northern part of Gaza through creating a closed circuit for wastewater treatment and reuse.

4. The accredited entity (AE) has requested €23.70 million in grant financing from GCF to finance project activities. Additional co-financing will be provided by Agence Française de Développement (AFD) and the World Bank.

5. The project has been categorized by the AE at a category II (medium-risk project) and by GCF as category A (high-risk project). The high-risk categorization of the project is due to the inclusion of archaeological sites in the project area, which may be affected by construction activities. The other key environmental and social risks relate to: (i) involuntary resettlement, which is made more likely because of the presence of archaeological sites; (ii) potential damage to buried artefacts, considering that the site is situated in an archaeologically important region; (iii) the increased risk of contamination from agricultural production.

6. The AE has conducted an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA)/environmental and social management plan (ESMP) and a resettlement action plan (RAP) along with the funding proposal to mitigate these risks.

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

The project's goal is to develop an integrated low-carbon water management scheme to reduce the demand for groundwater in Gaza by producing additional amounts of water usable for sustaining agriculture and increasing the resilience of local populations in Gaza. It will achieve this through the following outcomes:

(a) Reducing pressure on the coastal aquifer and securing sustainable access to domestic and agricultural water;

(b) Promoting climate-resilient and water-efficient agriculture; and

(c) Enhancing the institutional and operational capabilities for integrated and resilient water management.

Output 1: Production of additional quantities of water for agricultural use (total cost €4.13 million, co-financed by AFD).

The component will include activities to rehabilitate seven infiltration basins by transferring wastewater from the Beit Lahi wastewater treatment plant (WWTP) to the Northern Gaza coastal aquifer. The WWTP currently discharges treated wastewater into the coastal aquifer, resulting in a pollution plume underground and infiltrating basins closer to the sea. This project will transfer 10 million cubic metres of water per year from treated water through a network of 20 recovery wells located 500 meters downstream from the infiltration point and pipes connecting the wells to two reservoirs of 4,000 cubic metres each. The financing of the scheme was financed by the World Bank, covering €1 recovery well and one reservoir.


---
**Page 91**
---

## Output 2: Development of irrigation, water efficiency and climate resilient agriculture (Total cost: USD 503,849,000; Co-financing: USD 176,297,000; ODA net cost: USD 327,552,000)

### Output 2.1: Irrigation scheme (Total cost: USD 200 million; Co-financing: USD 80 million; ODA net cost: USD 120 million)

#### 9. The second part of the component will supply power to the NGST plant and the recovery boiler, which currently have restricted access to electricity from diesel generators.

The project will set up a total surface area of 8.5 ha of ground-mounted photovoltaic (PV) panels at three locations: i) adjacent to the NGST plant site; ii) adjacent to the recovery boiler site in the scheme (3 ha); and iii) adjacent to the NGST area (5.5 ha). The three systems will result in a total installed capacity of 10 MWp.

Electricity produced from these PV panels at the NGST plant site will supply power directly to the WWTP and the nearby sewage irrigation site (with only excess supply being supplied to the grid). Electricity from the third system will be delivered back to the system operator, which will then sell it on the grid. The project is expected to reduce direct electricity supply from the grid by 19 per cent. The AID has confirmed that land acquisition and compensation arrangements are in place with Israel authorities through existing coordination mechanisms already in place.

#### Output 2.2: Development of irrigation, water efficiency and climate resilient agriculture (Total cost: USD 303 million; Co-financing: USD 121 million; ODA net cost: USD 182 million)

### Output 2.3: Irrigation scheme (Total cost: USD 500 million; Co-financing: USD 200 million; ODA net cost: USD 300 million)

#### 10. This component consists of the delivery of an irrigation scheme over a gross irrigable area of 1,580 ha and agricultural services to the farmers/water users aimed at improving their practices in order to reduce water losses and increase on-farm productivity.

The component will deliver a distribution network to service two irrigated sub-areas of 500 ha and 1,000 ha gross irrigable area located around the WWTP, corresponding to a total of 126 km of pipeline and an upgrade of the booster station to support drip irrigation practices. On-farm infrastructure will be delivered in the form of water storage tanks (reimbursed with a subsidy rate) by the farmers over a three-year period in order to set up an operation and maintenance plan for these facilities.

The irrigation system will reduce water losses and expenditure, increase on-farm productivity and thereby improve food security. The project will also provide training services to farmers.

#### 11. This component will deliver a distribution network to service two irrigated sub-areas of 500 ha and 1,000 ha gross irrigable area located around the WWTP, corresponding to a total of 126 km of pipeline and an upgrade of the booster station to support drip irrigation practices.

On-farm infrastructure will be delivered in the form of water storage tanks (reimbursed with a subsidy rate) by the farmers over a three-year period in order to set up an operation and maintenance plan for these facilities. The irrigation system will reduce water losses and expenditure, increase on-farm productivity and thereby improve food security.

#### 12. This component will also support the creation of a WUA through the recovered water will be sold and delivered. It will also have the responsibility of distributing it equally to all users through an operation and maintenance plan for these facilities.

The irrigation system will reduce water losses and expenditure, increase on-farm productivity and thereby improve food security.

#### 13. This component will provide agricultural and extension services (i.e., technical and economic advisory services, capacity-building by demonstration, farmer field schools, training) to farmers in order to support the implementation of improved practices for efficient use of water, technical services and agricultural work. Thus, women will be engaged through community mobilization activities.

The project will also provide a revolving fund to support the creation of a Water Users Association (WUA) (in collaboration with local civil society organizations). This will increase the participation of women in the governance of the WUA and enable them to practice quality control over water use. The revolving fund will be used by farmers who are affiliated to cooperatives to market their produce and create food banks for families.

#### 14. This component will incorporate a gender-responsive approach to agricultural research to climate change, including the development of new technologies for efficient use of water, technical services and agricultural work. Thus, women will be engaged through community mobilization activities.

The project will also provide a revolving fund to support the creation of a Water Users Association (WUA) (in collaboration with local civil society organizations). This will increase the participation of women in the governance of the WUA and enable them to practice quality control over water use. The revolving fund will be used by farmers who are affiliated to cooperatives to market their produce and create food banks for families.

### Output 2.4: Management of the waste cycle and capacity-building of stakeholders (Total cost: USD 230 million; Co-financing: USD 186,750; ODA net cost: USD 43,250)


---
**Page 92**
---

1. The project will work towards improving institutional and governance mechanisms around water use and ensuring the sustained GMR of adaptation infrastructure. This component will support the capacity-building of relevant line agencies operating in Gaza through a technical assistance program to be implemented by the Palestinian Water Authority (PWA) to:
   * Coordinate the actions of implementing agencies;
   * Set up a monitoring and evaluation system to assess water quality for irrigation and broader project outcomes.
2. Stakeholder engagement will include beneficiaries, local communities, and other stakeholders (including beneficiaries);
3. Environmental and social management plan (ESMP), gender action plan (GAP) and resettlement action plan (RAP) implementation; and
4. Delivery of on-farm irrigation equipment.

5. The component will also include a monitoring and water quality control system to ensure the quality of water delivered to the irrigation scheme.
6. Finally, the component will include elements targeting social co-benefits to increase the adaptive capacity of beneficiaries through re-boosting community dynamics and knowledge-sharing.


### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
Scale: High

7. The 13 million cubic meters of water per year recovered through the WWTP and infiltration into the aquifer will reduce the exposure of around 2,553 farmers to climate risks by alleviating the pressure on the aquifer from agricultural activity.
8. In the context of extreme water scarcity, it is expected that this additional resource will free up approximately 10% of the total volume of groundwater that would otherwise be used for agriculture (in the baseline scenario). The project will meet the domestic needs of Gaza throughout the lifetime of the scheme. The total impact of the project therefore reaches 23,553 beneficiaries.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
Scale: High

9. Ingesting treated domestic wastewater within the water cycle creates a multiple effect, as a key adaptive response to water scarcity. The setup and strengthening of a water quality monitoring and control mechanism, which is paramount to the success of any reuse project, will ensure that this objective can be met.
10. The involvement of the Water Sector Regulatory Council as an external oversight body will be essential in limiting the risks of such projects and make sure that quality standards are met on a day-to-day basis.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential
Scale: High

11. The stronger inclusion of on-farm activities, a key aspect in the sustainability of reusing treated wastewater for irrigation purposes, is expected to be achieved through this project. This will set a precedent for a more holistic approach to the management of the water cycle.
12. The creation of WUA and the prioritization of women therein will lead to more equitable and gender-sensitive water use.


---
**Page 93**
---

1\. The activation of the water cycle downstream from the NGEST WWTP through the infiltration and recovery of treated wastewater will have two benefits in terms of reducing pollution and preventing the possible occurrence of a health crisis in Gaza City: (i) Infiltration of treated wastewater into the aquifer will reduce the amount of pollutants that are carried by surface runoff, and (ii) Recovery of treated wastewater from the NGEST WWTP caused by recovery will reverse the progression of the pollution plume towards the city wells.

2\. An environmental co-benefit of the project is the reduction in greenhouse gases through the mitigation of methane emissions.

3\. The project's gender action plan will ensure effective gender mainstreaming and the integration of a consistent gender perspective in the NGEST system in order to maximize climate and development co-benefits.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

1\. The food insecurity-water insecurity nexus is the main driver of vulnerability in Gaza, which highlights the importance of the present project. The reuse of treated wastewater through the recharge of the aquifer and wastewater recovery for agriculture results in the co-benefits of increased water availability and agricultural resilience.

### 3.5 Country ownership

**Scale: High**

1\. The project is well aligned with national climate plans and policies. The AE prepared this project proposal as part of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process, which includes an adaptation plan, which identifies enhancing the use of additional and alternative water resources for non-domestic purposes as one of its key areas.

2\. The project will contribute to the achievement of the SDGs by 2030, enhance resilience to climate change and increase adaptive capacity in 12 sectors, including water, wastewater and health. It clearly improves water security by creating new and additional resources for the country, which is greatly needed as it faces a severe water crisis. The project will also help the country respond to climate change challenges through enhanced aquifer management.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: High**

1\. The addition of PV fields to the NGEST system will reduce: (i) the annual direct supply from the grid in its first year of operation by 59 per cent; and (ii) the required annual energy from renewable sources for the same period by 60 per cent, which is equivalent to a saving of diesel fuel per year. Accordingly, the PV share in the energy mix feeding the NGEST system in the first year will be 12 per cent (diesel), 39 per cent (PV), and 49 per cent (biogas) compared with the rest supplied by diesel (10 per cent), grid electricity (30 per cent) and biogas (30 per cent).

2\. The NGEST WWTP + Recovery Scheme power supply without PV and with the current supply options leads to an overall levellized cost of energy (LCOE) of 0.24 USD/kilowatt hour (MWh), which is much higher than the LCOE for the same project when PV is installed, being much cheaper than the “no-PV situation”. This will generate savings amounting to USD 700,000 in the first year, together with a gain of USD 1.5 million from net metering (compensation of electricity sold back to the grid). The payback period for the PV fields is estimated at four years. In terms of the overall financial performance of the project, the gain should be around USD 360,000 in present value at a per cent discount rate.

# Environmental and social safeguards

## 4.1 Environmental and Social Risk Category

### (a) Environmental and Social Risk Category

The project is assessed as Category A given that it would result in significant adverse impacts on human health or the environment at a critical stage, although the interventions are partly designed to protect and improve the aquifer. There is a risk of further contamination of the groundwater due to malfunction or failure of the Scheme. The other factors considered in the assessment include the project's potential for social impact being more significant given the scarcity of land in the Gaza Strip, including displacement of existing private farmers and their families, as well as the vulnerability of the population to the effects of agricultural produce and exposure of consumers and farmworkers to these contaminants.

## 4.1 Environmental and Social Risk Category

### (b) Safeguard Instruments and Disclosure

The AE has submitted an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), and a Resettlement Action Plan (RAP). The documents are disclosed on the AE's and GEF's websites, respectively.

### (c) Compliance with GEF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards

The following summarizes the project’s compliance to GEF ESS standards and requirements:

#### (i) ESS 1: Access to Information and Public Participation in Decision-Making on Project-Related Impacts. The AE has prepared an ESMP, which is an updated version of the ESIA for the NGEST Phase B. The ESIA covers the expansion and the additional components of the project. The ESIA includes a detailed environmental impact assessment focused on the Managed Aquifer Recharge (MAR) scheme using partially treated wastewater as recharge water to improve groundwater quality in the aquifer to sustain existing and additional water abstraction for irrigation. The recovery component of the project will be implemented after the completion of the MAR infiltrates that had accumulated in the aquifer during the early periods of NGEST when raw sewage was transferred into the infiltration basis while the WWTP was not yet operational.

#### (ii) ESS 2: Labor and Working Conditions. Around 150 workers are expected to be involved in the project, including those working on construction, operation, safety risks and job-related accidents, illness, and other adverse impacts on workers. Influx of contractors will also have an impact on local utilities and social-dynamics. To manage this, the project will orient the workers and be required to comply with a Code of Conduct governing workers’ behaviour during off-site work. The Code of Conduct will include measures for health and safety at work that also included measures to manage labour and working conditions of the project including recruitment, training, remuneration, and other aspects related to labour and exploitation.

#### (iii) ESS 3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention. The project will maximize resource efficiency by treating and reusing wastewater for irrigation. It will also facilitate the removal of a plume of low-quality infiltrates currently in the aquifer, which had accumulated during NGEST when raw sewage was transferred into the infiltration basis while NGEST WWTP was not yet operational.

#### (iv) ESS 4: Community Health, Safety and Security. The project will ensure recovered wastewater for irrigation is safe to use by testing it before being used for irrigated farming especially during the early years of operation. Farm workers may also be exposed to pathogens in the treated water. These risks are expected to be mitigated by addressing them through regular monitoring of irrigation water quality and restricting the types of crops grown on land irrigated with recovered wastewater. The ESIA indicated that a security plan is aligned to the Voluntary Principles on Security and Human Rights. The AE also commits to develop a revised and comprehensive


---
**Page 95**
---

### ESS 5: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement

A Resettlement Action Plan (RAP) which adequately addressed all lands and ROW requirements of the proposed facilities has been developed.

The construction of 14 reservoirs and five (5) monitoring wells will require the acquisition of small portions of land from 20 households who are directly affected by the project. In addition, 39 community wells in the area. The RAP has identified a total of 39 households who will be directly affected by the project in particular, 12 private agricultural wells will be closed after construction and compensation for 16 landowners will be provided for small portions of their lands due to the construction of the Wells. The 30 dunams (c.a. 3 hectares) of land that will be acquired from the Ministry of Agriculture will be compensated by the Ministry of Endowment (Waqf) land. No further land acquisition is required for the drip irrigation system and the water distribution network.

The project will not require any compensation to the farmers who will be displaced as a result of the construction of the wells. The 12 households affected by the project will be installed in the main roads and areas. Temporary access roads at the farm during the construction period will be provided for the affected households. In addition, on the consultations with the PAPs, the 11 well operators will be provided with permanent access to their wells.

The compensation for the landowners who are displaced by the project will be based on alternative sources of water for their farms. The 15 landowners had opted to sell the whole of their affected parcels at the market price. The land taken from the Waqf will be reclassified as agricultural land.

### ESS 6: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources

The project is expected not to significantly impact the habitat of these species as the project facilities will generally be located in the built-up areas, and the construction of the reservoirs and wells will not result in any significant changes. The project will serve only existing farms and will not open up new areas for cultivation.

### ESS 7: Indigenous People

The ESIA reported that there are no indigenous peoples in the project area.

### ESS 8: Cultural Heritage

The project will not affect any monuments, historical or cultural sites of national importance. In addition to the field surveys of the ESIA did not identify any archaeological sites within the project area. This is also confirmed by the Antiquity Authority (AA), the minimal excavation involved, and the fact that no significant changes are expected in the built-up areas. The AA has agreed to clarify these arrangements at the project inception.

### Implementation Arrangements

The EIB will be responsible for the project implementation and activities carried out by the EEs, namely, the PRAA and RAO. A project management unit (PMU) within the PWA will also be established. The project will also engage an Environmental Management Unit (EMU). The Egyptian Environment Quality Authority (EEQA), is also involved in its capacity as NDA and a partner in the water quality monitoring network. In view of the above, the institutional arrangements for the implementation of the ESMF need to be clarified based on the proposed PMU structure and the roles and responsibilities of various agencies (e.g., Central Management Unit, PWA, EEQA). The ESIA has agreed that the draft document clarifies these arrangements at project inception.


---
**Page 96**
---

10. Stakeholder engagement: The stakeholders for NGST have been continuously engaged during project preparation. Additional consultations have been conducted among beneficiaries of the irrigation system and those adversely affected to enable the various stakeholders to participate in the decision-making process at all stages from concept implementation phases. A Multi-Stakeholder Engagement Plan (MSEP) for the construction and implementation phases has been prepared as part of the project. The MSEP provides for the hiring of a Social Inclusion Officer (SIO) who is responsible for stakeholder engagement and relationship management and will facilitate information flow and build relationships with the communities affected by the project. The SIO will be updated and revised on a quarterly basis during the construction and operation phases of the project.

10. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM): In addition to the GRM at the entity level, the project will also implement a project-level GRM to provide local residents with a means of consistently addressing grievances related to the project in an efficient manner. At the project level, grievances will be handled by the PMU or the PWA. The SIO is designated as the focal point for the project-level GRM and will work closely with the district municipalities, to handle grievances related to resettlement activities. A grievance mechanism will also be established for workers with a proper communication channels to enable workers to voice concerns, particularly on workers' health and safety.

### 4.2 Gender policy

17. The AII has submitted a gender assessment; therefore, it complies with the operational guidelines of the GCF gender policy and action plan. The AII has also provided a gender action plan for the project:

- **Gender equity:** is a persistent challenge in Gaza despite improvements in gender equity over the last decade, as illustrated in the gender analysis. The analysis indicates the existence of significant gender inequalities in terms of access to resources, including perceptions and attitudes towards women and the existence of adverse social norms towards women. Further laws on inheritance affect women's access to land and property, whereby Gazan women do not have equal rights with men regarding ownership or inheritance.

- **The assessment also indicates that social and institutional structures are weakened due to conflict, which has a negative impact on women's ability to participate in the household work and care; furthermore, they reinforce the existing lack of access to financial resources for women.**

18. The assessment was undertaken through desk review as well as stakeholder consultations whereby the views and opinions of both women and men were solicited. The consultation process included focus group discussions with women and men on their social and reproductive roles as well as access to resources such as land and irrigated water. The assessment also involved interviews with key informants, including local authorities and women. Women spend most of their time fulfilling household responsibilities while men work on farms and in the construction sector. Women are responsible for managing household finances and decision-making power. Women however do have critical roles in food production, post-harvest activities, livestock care, and increasingly in cash cropping, spending 34% of their day working on these activities compared to only 10% by men. Men spend more time than women on income-related marketing activities. Access to irrigated water is low for both women and men, but even lower for women who are less likely to have access to irrigation systems.

- **Irrigation is further exacerbated by their lack of technical knowledge and skills about irrigation. During the dry season, women collect rainwater in containers or buckets and use recovered water to irrigate their farms and plots, if it is affordable. More importantly however, because few women formally own land, their participation and representation

# Risks

## Overall proposal assessment (medium risk)

### 4.3 Risks

#### Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute (medium risk)

- **AFD**: AFD is the AF for the project. The AFD Group has operated in the Palestinian Territories since 1998 and has financed approximately 50 projects by 2017, representing a total of EUR 346 million.
  
- **The project has two executing entities: PWA and PWB**: PWBA has been involved in other projects funded by the World Bank, AFD and Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau (KfW). PWBA is a public company that was established to implement the Water Sector Reform Program. The Ministry of Agriculture will coordinate with PWBA and PWA on the implementation of defining water sector policy and conducting the reform process.
  
- **The Ministry of Agriculture**: The MOA has been operating in the West Bank and Gaza Strip since 2002 and has three offices there. FAO has recently worked on the use of reclaimed wastewater for irrigation systems proposed through the project.

# Project-specific risks (high risk)

## Regulatory support: The project leverages the success of the earlier project funded by the World Bank and AFD – the NEST wastewater treatment plant. The project will build infrastructure to treat wastewater from this plant, which will be used for irrigation in the vicinity of the WWTF. The first part of this investment (14 recovery wells) is already under construction. The second phase of this investment (14 remaining wells and an additional 20 km of pipeline) has been approved by the Palestinian government. Under the Water Law (updated in 2013), the AE has expressed the view that enforcing regulations on the quality of water in the aquifer will be key challenges of the project.

### Security concerns: Technical and feasibility studies implemented for the preparation of the groundwater management plan indicate that there is a risk of “sink and go” due to the intermittent degradation of the security conditions in Gaza. Such issues may also affect project execution and costs. The AE also acknowledged the possibility of such security issues affecting the project.

### Water tariff: The project will implement the creation of a WUA, which will be responsible for distribution of water by farmers by levying a transfer fee. It is necessary that the tariff conforms to farmers’ willingness and ability to pay. This will be realized based on the results of the feasibility study. The feasibility study conducted in 2013 indicated that the analysis done during the feasibility study demonstrate the capacity of pay of farmers, as well as their willingness to pay for water (economic, social, cultural and/or political). The AE stated that a further study on this will be conducted when the project is secured and underway.

### Ownership: The Palestinian government of the State of Palestine will initially bear the O&M costs, with farmers subsequently carrying the responsibility. On-farm equipment will be rented from the WUA, which will be responsible for distribution of water to the irrigation scheme (managed by WUA), which will be a key factor in the long-term viability of the project.

### Economic viability: The economic model estimates the direct benefits of the project (mainly from output 2 [agriculture/irrigation]) over 30 years. The direct benefits of the GCF project are estimated at an internal rate of return of 5 per cent. The AE opined that the economic internal rate of return (EIRR) will be very high post-implementation, considering the indirect economic and social co-benefits.

## Compliance risk (medium)

### Project activities pertaining to construction pose risks to procurement integrity. However, the AE has implemented those typically expected in procurement-related activities. Furthermore, the AE has well-established systems and controls in place to ensure effective risk prevention.

### Political vulnerabilities: There are political vulnerabilities that may affect the project’s timely execution. The AE highlights its extensive experience in navigating the uncertainties through


---
**Page 99**
---

reliance on close collaboration with bilateral agencies in order to facilitate smooth implementation.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low)

54. In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of result area and single proposal is not material.
55. It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

16. The executing entities for the project are PWA and FAO:
- As an executing entity in the project, the PWA will be the lead implementing agency for the project and will be in charge of the overall coordination of activities, except for on-farm development activities.
- In order to ensure appropriate coordination between stakeholders of the project and supervise the following on a non-objection basis: (i) signature of the on-granting agreement between PWA and FAO; (ii) signing of financial agreements with FAO; and (iii) tendering process.
- A project management unit established within the project will be responsible for carrying out all tending activities for capital investments, recruiting international technical assistance for project management support, and ensuring the financial management of the project. The PWA and project management unit will sign a non-obligatory agreement with FAO to provide services as an international TA.
- The Ministry of Finance and Planning, as the legal representative of the State of Palestine for signing financial agreements with AFI, will sign an on-granting agreement with PWA.

## Results monitoring and reporting

The GEF framework has been revised in line with the IGF project management framework in order to ensure that all relevant indicators are captured and met. In the project covers both adaptation and mitigation aspects, relevant indicators have been included.

The greenhouse gas emission calculation approach has been revised and applies methods consistent with those used by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change's Clean Development Mechanism methodologies. The approach is conservative and allows for a transparent greenhouse gas monitoring and estimation.

The implementation timeline was also revised to reflect the relevant milestones during project implementation.


## Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 11 November 2017, and it became effective on 16 January 2018 (the "AMA").

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all necessary approvals to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the State of Palestine, a country in which the GEF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, the GEF cannot sue or otherwise enforce its rights against the government of the State if it is found to have committed an act of terrorism and/or expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both stated that it would not be legally feasible for them to undertake their redress activities against the State of Palestine. The GEF is therefore unable to provide the State with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GEF are made subject to the following conditions.


## List of proposed conditions (including legal)

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:
