# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP119

## Proposal name: Water Ranking and Adaptation of Agriculture to Climate Change in Northern Gaza

### Accredited entity: Agence Française de Développement (AFD)

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: Medium

#### 1.1 Adaptation impact

##### 1.1.1 Adaptation impact

1. **Project activities are divided into three main components:**  
   (a) Managed aquifer recharge with treated wastewater, followed by a recovery and reuse scheme, including a photovoltaic (PV) power plant to feed the system;  
   (b) Implementation of drip irrigation systems on approximately 2,000 ha of agricultural land for irrigation, and the installation of off-farm drip irrigation equipment and provision of extension services to farmers related to climate-resilient agriculture, crop diversification, and water management practices;  
   (c) Capacity building with the Palestinian Water Authority (PWA) and water user associations, and support for community empowerment and knowledge management to promote replication and upscaling.

2. **Direct beneficiaries:** Farmers would substantially benefit from the installation of the drip irrigation systems and increased water availability for irrigation include farm owners and seasonal workers. The number of direct beneficiaries should be estimated as households rather than people. Increased demand for seasonal and permanent labour should be considered an economic benefit. Given that the project would benefit 1,052 households in total, it is estimated that approximately 3,846 direct beneficiaries could be estimated at 3,532 people.

3. **As per the baseline scenario:** Once fully implemented, the recovered water would be sufficient to cover over 100 per cent of the crop irrigation requirements in the target area.

4. **Long-term benefits:** The project will provide the revitalization of nine existing infiltration basins, which would receive the treated effluent from the new North Gaza wastewater treatment plant (WWTP). The infiltration would take place through sandy soil and therefore act as a natural filter for the water quality (Nitrification), and sediment in the shallow section of the aquifer will then be extracted by a set of 28 recovery wells placed 500 m downstream from the WWTP. This will allow the treated wastewater to percolate into the groundwater, where it can be used as an additional source of water flow to be managed according to demand from farmers, a distribution network to deliver water to agricultural land and off-farm drip irrigation equipment to cover 2,000 ha.


---
**Page 103**
---

1. As a by-stage of the project, the construction of 14 recovery wells, a 4,000 m³ storage tank and the rehabilitation of the first two basins has already been completed through financing from the World Bank.
2. By providing water for irrigation, the project would relieve the pressure on other groundwater resources, indirectly allowing an increase in the net aquifer recharge and therefore improving access to domestic water for approximately 200,000 people currently vulnerable to water scarcity.
3. Although drought is an issue that could certainly be exacerbated by climate change, it is clear that the current state of water resources and infrastructure in the Gaza Strip is not mainly caused by this phenomenon.
4. Nevertheless, the regional climate model PRECIP (Providing Regional Climate for Impact Projections) under a high emissions scenario (A1B), projects a decrease in precipitation of 15 per cent at 23 per century to 2100, respectively, which would lower the per capita internal water resources in the State of Palestine by approximately 40%.
5. The Palestinian Ministry of Environment's Programme’s analysis of climate vulnerability in the State of Palestine highlights climate risks as a humanitarian issue.
6. The State of Palestine's fragile water supply infrastructure and the growing water scarcity will worsen agricultural yields and exacerbate current high levels of food and water insecurity, leading to further population displacement, increased social unrest and conflict, and generating general unrest in the population. The total annual groundwater extraction in the Gaza Strip is estimated at 105 million cubic meters per year (mcm/yr). However, the aquifer does not exceed 60 mcm/yr. As a result, the water table is rapidly declining, promoting salinization of agricultural land and desertification in the area, already heavily polluted by fertilizers, pesticides and untreated sewage.
7. As a result of the delay on the construction of the North Gaza WWTP and the general socio-economic situation in the Gaza Strip, the population has been using the aquifer to infiltrate the aquifer through the existing infiltration basins, creating an underground pollution plume.
8. Although the proposal states that the use of the recovered water would be unrestricted, data on the environmental and social impact assessment (ESAs) of the reuse and recovery scheme show that the proposed wastewater treatment and recovery scheme would be installed showed that, in general, groundwater quality parameters currently comply with the Palestinian standards set by the Ministry of Environment and the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). However, 15% of the wells with initial concentrations above the maximum mercury levels exceeding the permissible Palestinian standards. All borehole levels were within the recommended range of 0.7–2 mg/L Mercury. On the other hand, was present in values up to 10 times the recommended limit for agricultural use. The FAO recommends that the use of agricultural soil with treated wastewater containing an average mercury concentration of 0.056 g/kg should be avoided and that the use of such soils is only permitted under conditions of up to 500 mg/kg, which is 10 times higher than the accepted limit for mercury in agricultural


---
**Page 104**
---

soil of 0.9 ng/g Mercury concentrations on the tested wells ranged from 0.001 to 483 ng/g, which represents between 2 and 20 times the concentration of treated wastewater in Pling et al.'s study.

12. Questioned about this issue, the project proponent responded that the seven monitoring wells tested for heavy metals are located closer to the infiltration basins than to the recovery wells and that for this reason, the concentration of pollutants is higher than expected on the recovery wells. The project proponent also stated that the groundwater quality of the recovery wells has not been tested for at the recovery well.

13. In addition, analyses of samples taken from the recovery wells showed that sodium levels exceeded the drinking water limit in all samples; nitrate-nitrogen levels exceeded these limits in approximately 50 per cent of the wells, and chloride and potassium levels in some cases were also above the drinking water limits.

14. The "Public Health Related Monitoring Plan for Using Recovery Water" described in the ESSA of the Reuse and Recovery Scheme,1 which defines the parameters that would need to be periodically monitored on the recovery well, does not explicitly include mercury, boron or sodium. Clearly, the project design underestimates the potential contamination of groundwater by these pollutants.

15. Given the baseline scenario, the monitoring plan explicitly includes testing for boron, mercury and sodium in longer-term scenarios. In the short term, however, if the recovery wells are used, they must also be periodically tested for in soil and plants in order to monitor potential secondary impacts.

16. Organic contaminants, although specified under the required periodic analyses in the monitoring plan," were not measured during project design when the water quality of the aquifer or groundwater was assessed.

17. The original monitoring plan proposed in this funding proposal had a monitoring frequency for all parameters of four times a year; except for the water level, the measurement frequency would be reduced to once every six months after the first year. It is expected that a complete assessment of groundwater quality must be carried out on the recovery wells before the intended use of the recovered water as drinking water can be considered.

18. Parameters exceeding or close to permissible limits shall continue to be monitored on a monthly basis until they reach acceptable limits and show a decreasing trend.

19. As explained in the project proponent's response to question 5, "the deputation of the aquifer is expected to take place by the joint effects of two mechanisms: - the dilution effect of the recovered water which will be introduced into the aquifer via the ITAP on the matter," - the depuration of the aquifer is expected to take place by the joint effects of two mechanisms: - the dilution effect of the recovered water which will be introduced into the aquifer via the ITAP on the matter, and the aspiration effect of the functioning recovery well, which will lead to the slowing down of the recharge rate. The project proponent explained that if the volume of recovered water use would be partially or totally restricted, and that it is expected that farmers would only gradually change from private wells to the new water supply system, the extraction rate need to prevent over-extraction of groundwater in the aquifer will be reduced considerably smaller than the ideal rate of almost 40,000 m³/day." The underground model assumed a maximum volume of recovered water use of approximately 15,000 m³/day. The "aspiration effect" of the recovery wells in the depuration process." The proponent explained that to ensure the dilution and depuration effects of the recovered well, excess water from the recovery wells may be re-filtered in the aquifer in order to accelerate dilution and, at the same time,

# Maximizing water activity

Nevertheless, it is not clear if the infiltration basin would have the capacity to receive and infiltrate that extra flow of excess water. For these reasons, the EAP considers there to be a risk of failing to achieve the required extraction rate, resulting in the expansion of the project beyond its recovery well into agricultural and domestic wells.

## Mitigation impact

1. **Mitigation measure**: The project will include a Palestinian/Jordanian groundwater monitoring plan (WQMP), based on the Palestinian/Jordanian guidelines for irrigation water. For heavy metals, a stricter standard is applied (i.e., 0.5 mg/l of lead in drinking water and 2 mg/l of lead in irrigation water) in order to follow a zero risk policy. These standards and the WQMP are presented in annex II.

The laboratory and related equipment to be financed through the project will increase the monitoring autonomy of the PWA and allow thorough and intensified monitoring of water quality parameters (e.g., heavy metals, nitrates, pesticides) at all stages of the extraction and distribution of water to farmers. Once the laboratory is up and running, the WQMP will be put into place.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

#### Scale: High

The evaluation of project outcomes and the generation of knowledge products to capitalize on lessons learned and enable replication and upscaling are incorporated under activity 3.1 with an assigned budget of EUR 300,000. However, the specifics of this output are not described in detail.

It is the first implementation of managed aquifer recharge and it will be the first time a reuse scheme has been implemented at scale (i.e., 25 million m³/year) in the State of Palestine. For this reason, and being that the rest of the State of Palestine faces similar challenges and baseline scenarios, the project has considerable replication and upscaling potential.

## On the feasibility study

The gross irrigation water requirement of the targeted agricultural land is estimated at approximately 13 million m³/year (Eurostat data). In 80.2% of the time, the recovery well with a capacity of 500 m³/day will be able to extract this amount of water in less than ten per hour from the uppermost aquifer plane, which is the only one that can be used for irrigation purposes. The remaining 19.8% of the time, the recovery well will be able to extract approximately 4 million m³/year (which is 3 million m³/year in excess of the estimated gross irrigation requirement).

25. The sustainability of the project beyond project lifespan rests with the exit strategy included in Activity 3.2, which involves the following scheme: PWA would own the recovery and reuse schemes and would be responsible for its operation and maintenance (O&M) during the first five years after project completion. After this period, the Water Reclamation Sector Company will be functional and ready to take over the O&M of the recovery scheme, and the water users associations will be capable of taking responsibility for the O&M of the reuse scheme.

24. Users are expected to pay a monthly tariff that would be sufficient to cover the O&M costs of the project beyond its lifespan. The cost is shared between the water users' associations. The required tariff was calculated to be 0.61 ILS per cubic meter (m3).**[1]** The funding proposal states that the Water Reclamation Sector Company will be responsible for the operation of the project with the support of FAO.*[2]* However, the feasibility study*[3]* mentions that a survey indicated that, based on the cost of the diesel used to extract water, farmers pay, on average, ILS 1.5 per cubic meter. The feasibility study also states that, in addition to the water supply system, the proponent expects that all farmers would eventually switch to the new reclaimed water system.

25. Consulted on the financing of on-farm drip irrigation, which farm owners are expected to repay over a period of five years and which would constitute the O&M fund for the water recovery and reuse scheme beyond project completion. The repayment includes the repayment of on-farm equipment.*[4]* This tariff is calculated in the tariff and organization plan (TAP) document. The TAP also states that the farm owners will cover 50% of O&M of the recovery and reuse system, including the PV facility, as estimated in the economic model for the project beyond its lifespan. The TAP further states that the farm owners will cover the cost of on-farm equipment.*[4]* In response to a question raised by the TAP on this issue, the FA indicated that there was no need to include the cost of on-farm equipment in the tariff because these were defined or reflected the scenario that would prevail after a full tariff study (to be performed during the first phase of the project) and negotiations with involved parties. The AE also responded by stating that the TAP document is not final, and that further discussion/negotiation between parties – including the farmers – were central to the work the project team was doing and its technical assessment would need to perform throughout the first months of the project.

26. As the proposed tariff assures that all farmers would adopt the new water supply system beyond project completion, it is expected that the financial sustainability of the project beyond the proposed tariff will depend on achieving high adherence by farmers.

27. According to the feasibility study*[3]* and the TAP document,*[4]* it is expected that farmers would prefer to use conventional well water, because it can potentially save money owing to reduced fertilizer use. To allow for this, it is paramount that nutrient testing on recovered water be conducted in advance of project implementation. The same information is evaluated along with crop requirements to advise farmers on specific fertilizer needs.

### Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

The responsibilities of the Water Sector Regulatory Council (WSRC) include, among others, the approval of water tariffs, costs of supply networks and other related services, monitoring of service providers' performance, ensuring that all water supply network service providers with adopted standards, setting quality assurance standards, and establishment of a database for technical, financial and statistical information. The project would strengthen the capacity of WSRC to participate in the steering and the technical committees in order to allow it to control project investments.

## Sustainable development potential

### Environmental and social co-benefits

In the present project, environmental and social co-benefits and hazards are closely related, since the affected environment consists mainly on the aquifer and the aquifer is the main domestic water source for the population of the coastal area. The groundwater model shows that after the installation of the first stage of recovery wells, the groundwater table level at the coastline in the same area as the infiltration basins would be reduced by about 10 meters. This reduction would not only reduce the potential risk of promoting saline intrusion, the proponent explained that this reduction could be due to the marginal increase in the water table level in the coastal aquifer and the consequent mass towards the sea - though slowed down by the implementation of the recovery wells – which was demonstrated during the feasibility study for the first stage of the desalination project. The AED added that this would be closely monitored and the intensity of pumping to manage both the deposition co-benefit and adequate quantities extracted for agriculture would be fine-tuned.

The most important environmental co-benefit consists of the gradual remediation that will occur in the aquifer, as a result of the infiltration of treated wastewater. The infiltration of treated wastewater, and the consequent dilution of the contaminated groundwater. As suggested in the groundwater model, the pollution plume present in the area of the main infiltration basins would be reduced by about 10 meters below the surface of the basins; would be greatly reduced after seven years of water infiltration and recovery. It should be noted that this is highly dependent on the capacity of the desalination plant.

The extracted groundwater consists of the pollution plume that has been cleaned from the aquifer, gradually diluted by the infiltrated treated wastewater. As explained in paragraphs 8-10 above, some groundwater quality parameters in the area of the recovery wells have higher values than those recommended for drinking water and irrigation purposes. The most important problem seems to be the potential mercury content and salinity. When reclaimed water fails comply with Palestinian standards for irrigation water, partial or total restrictions should be applied, to avoid soil contamination and potential environmental and health hazards.


---
**Page 108**
---

14. Mercury is harmful even at low concentrations to plants, animals and humans, and accumulates because of its long biological half-life. In plants, mercury stunts seedling growth and root development and inhibits photosynthesis. In humans, it has been demonstrated to affect the central nervous system, impacting the neurological, psychomotor and intellectual development of children.

13.2 Economic co-benefits

The increased productivity of agriculture activities due to project outcomes would generate an increase in income for permanent and seasonal workers, which, as noted previously, would result in indirect economic benefits.

Another economic co-benefit could be linked to the improved health situation of the population. The reduction of contamination levels in water resources and soils caused by underground pollution would prevent the contamination plume reaching other agricultural and domestic wells, therefore maintaining a higher groundwater quality with subsequent positive effects on agriculture and improving public health resource systems.

13.3 Gender-sensitive development impact

The project would support the empowerment of women mostly through the promotion of female membership and leadership in the water users associations, which would be responsible for the management of the water resources. This would allow women to obtain opportunities or mechanisms for women to obtain land ownership.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient
**Scale: High**

#### 1.4.1 Vulnerability of the country, vulnerable groups and gender aspects

The State of Palestine is highly vulnerable to the effects of climate change, especially drought given its limited water resources and economic situation.

Groundwater availability in Gaza Strip has become increasingly limited because the natural recharge rate from rainfall is not sufficient to replenish the groundwater extracted by wells at the Eastern and Northern Gaza Borders. Also, the dams constructed in Israel along the upper stream of the Wadi Al-Arba'eh have depleted the natural flow of the Gaza Strip, making water scarcity worse in the arid strip. The decline of the groundwater level is exacerbated per capita consumption; the Palestinian per capita consumption is between 15 and 80 l/day (for below the age of five) compared to the Israeli standard at 169 l/day), while the Israeli per capita consumption exceeds 300 l/day.

In addition, perhaps equally significant is the low rainfall (200 mm/400 mm/year) and the fact that with approximately 5.8 million inhabitants/cap., the population density in the Gaza Strip is one of the highest in the world.


### Economic and social development

**Page 109**
---

1.4.3 Absence of alternative sources of financing

The funding proposal clearly describes the challenging economic and social situation of the Gaza Strip, which is mainly a result of war and restrictions on trade and access to resources imposed by Israel.

### 1.4.4 Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

#### 1.4.4.1 The PWA has already received training during the implementation of the Northern Gaza Emergency Sewage Treatment (NGEST) system and associated projects, and the present proposal will build on this experience to strengthen the PWA during the five years of project lifespan, to guarantee knowledge transfer and PWA capacity to manage the new recovery water treatment plant. The PWA will be responsible for training project implementers and stakeholders, development and implementation of the water quality control system, preparation of the operation and maintenance manual, preparation of the technical management plan, the resettlement action plan and the gender action plan, and the delivery of on-farm irrigation equipment.

#### 1.4.4.2 Water recovery systems, which is essential to achieve the social and environmental benefits that the project seeks, would involve the development and implementation of a monitoring system for the quality of recovered water, including the amount of treated water infiltrated into the aquifer and the recovered water to be delivered for irrigation. The system would include 15 monitoring wells equipped with monitoring probes, 3 of which will be financed by AFD. The PWA will also be responsible for training staff to support the upgrading of the laboratory of the North Gaza WWTP, including staff training.

### 1.5 Country ownership

#### Scale: High

##### 1.5.1 Alignment with national climate strategy and policies

The present proposal is fully aligned with initial national communication report and was included as part of the National Adaptation Plan to Climate Change 2014 as an adaptation alternative to improve the use of alternative water resources for non-domestic purposes in the Gaza Strip.

##### 1.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver

AFD has already financed 13 water and sanitation investment programs in the State of Palestine for a total amount of nearly EUR 100 million, benefiting an estimated 800,000 people.

The executing entities will be the PWA, the Ministry of Agriculture and FAO. The weaknesses of PWA are identified in the proposal and would be addressed through the capacity-building activities described above.

##### 1.5.3 Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

As explained in the proposal, PWA in coordination with the Palestinian Environment Quality Authority are the main promoters of the project since its inception.

## Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: medium

### Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

1. **Cost-effectiveness** and efficiency

2. In general, budget allocation and financial structure seem adequate for the proposed outputs.

3. However, the cost estimation of drip irrigation systems was made as if the cost of implementing such a system would be similar to that of implementing sprinkler irrigation. When implementing drip irrigation for grains or vegetables can cost as much as two or three times that of other crops, and considering the current exchange rate (approximately IJ 2.50/€), the expected cost is in the upper range for grains and vegetables. As explained in the proposal, it is estimated at EUR 1,300/ha. With this logic, the cost of the first stage of 500 ha was estimated at EUR 1,300/ha, and for the second stage of 1,000 ha, where farms are larger, the cost was estimated at EUR 2,800/ha.

### Amount of co-financing

4. The project would be co-financed by GCF, DFID, and Irish Aid, which would contribute with 53%, 37% and 10% cost share, respectively.

### Financial viability

5. While the funding proposal states that the proposed tariff would be USD $0.33/ha., which at the current exchange rate represents approximately IJ 2.50/€, the balance sheets for different crops, where the net margin for each crop is expected to cover a scenario of 0.6/ha. or more, were developed in line with the pre-project scenarios. Although various tariffs were considered during the development of the present proposal, the proposal clarified that the tariff would be based on the average cost per hectare during the first phase of the project and negotiations with involved parties.

6. The economic model shows a cost-benefit analysis considering the benefits of the new irrigation system only if there are an agreement to cover the balance sheet developed for each crop. If some crops which have negative net margin


---
**Page 111**
---

would still be implanted in both baseline and project scenarios, at a loss. For example, in the baseline scenario used in the economic model, olive trees have a net margin of -ILS 1,571/damum). This estimation assumes a labour cost of ILS 320/damum. If labour cost is not considered, then the net margin would be negative ILS 489/damum. When questioned by the ITAP on this matter, the proponent responded: "It is indeed expected that some farmers will lose money in the short term and others will pursue the cultivation of crops that show individual negative margins. These are generally strategic decisions made at a farm level (with generally private incentives associated). Farms are systemic units and, generally, low or negative margin crops are balanced by higher margin crops (e.g. vegetables and citrus) which are more resilient to climate change and water scarcity processes.

1. Based on the information provided in the funding proposal, the economic model set out in this proposal presents multiple uncertainties that should be clarified during the initial stages of project implementation. However, the public service nature of the project and the importance and urgency of its objectives, which are directly related to climate change adaptation, leads the ITAP to believe that the project should proceed with a preliminary economic assessment.

# Gender Action Plan

## 1. Introduction

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) consolidates and synthetically presents the information learned from the Gender Analysis in order to maximize the recovery scheme's impacts on the improvement of gender issues in the project area. As such, this GAP is the key gender mainstreaming mechanism for ensuring gender-inclusiveness during operation of the NGEST project. The aim of the GAP is to provide a framework for the implementation of the project by designing tangible and explicit actions and indicators for the operation phase. The aim is to promote opportunities, drivers of change and positive gender dynamics as well as to manage and mitigate risks related to gender in the project area. The GAP also ensures that the program is compliant with GGC gender policy (GGC/B/09/12).

The Gender Action Plan provides suggested entry points for gender-responsive actions to be taken under each activity of the NGEST project. In addition, specific indicators are proposed to measure and track progress on these actions at the activity level. This will be incorporated into the Project Monitoring and Evaluation Framework (PMEF). The Gender Action Plan also provides concrete recommendations on how to ensure information on gender (including disaggregated data) continues to be collected and measured throughout implementation. A gender-specific budget has been allocated for project activities.

The GAP is closely aligned to the outputs of the logic framework and planned activities of the recovery scheme, as well as the environmental components of the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) that already contain gender-related aspects.1 In addition to the specific activities of the GAP, the NGEST project will apply measures in accordance with the Palestinian Water Sector Reform Plan2 as well as the National Water and Wastewater Strategy for Palestine.

The National Water and Wastewater Strategy for Palestine ensures the adequate involvement of all stakeholders (from a gender perspective) in water and wastewater programs & projects to support sustainable use of water resources management in Palestine. The gap analysis will be used by the different stakeholders involved in the NGEST project team, NGEST operator, the Project Coordination Unit (PCU), the Palestinian Water Authority (PWA), the Coastal Municipalities Water Utility (CMWU), water services providers and other relevant stakeholders.

**Page 118**

providers and other relevant entities that will be established in the future, such as the Water User Association (WUA).

The GAP takes into consideration that the water for irrigation in the project area is from a reclaimed source from the NGEST project. Utilizing treated municipal wastewater for Managed Aquifer Recharge and then extracting that groundwater for agriculture is essential to food security in rural areas of Gaza and North Gaza. The recovered groundwater will contribute significantly to increasing agricultural productivity, especially on lands that are currently rainfed lands to irrigated lands, which will increase the agriculture productivity of these lands. In addition, there are 2300 dunums of uncultivated areas due to water scarcity, so the irrigation scheme would encourage cultivation of these lands as well.

Policy and decision-making regarding land and water management have traditionally been the domain of men in rural communities; participation does not always consider women’s unique knowledge, needs, or unequal ownership rights. Women farmers need to be actively involved in the planning and implementation of water management programs and must be able to participate fully in developing the policies that affect their access and control of these resources. This GAP presents a number of proposed activities based on the experience and lessons of gender-analysis.

### Objective

The GAP intends to increase participation by women in associated agricultural activities, particularly through community-based organizations and water users associations; more equitable access to project and program resources including skills training, technology, and services; improved land tenure security for women; increased income generation opportunities; food security; and more livelihood options; progress toward gender equality, including changing decision-making patterns in the household, membership in and leadership capacity within community-based organizations.

Gender equity and women’s empowerment are essential for meeting the direct beneficiaries’ aspirations of inclusive and sustainable development. Gender equality needs to be pursued in its own right for a just and equal society, and for better development outcomes. This GAP includes clear targets, gender design features and quantifiable performance indicators to ensure men and women participation and benefits.

### Methodology

#### 3.1 Identify and design gender elements for the NGEST recovery scheme
- Use the Gender Analysis to understand the specific vulnerabilities within the target population, in terms of social roles, needs, uses and access to services, priorities, decision-making, participation in decision making, and impact of access to water on women and men.

4


---
**Page 119**
---

Based on the vulnerabilities assessed, identify locally appropriate gender elements to be targeted.

Once identified, design how the gender elements that have been targeted can be included in the recovery scheme activities.

Targets and strategies should enable step-by-step progress, bringing incremental changes with each phase of implementation and mainstreaming it.

Help relevant stakeholders understand the rationale for focusing on women.


### 3.2 Select outputs, activities, targets and indicators

* The Gender Action plan provides suggested entry points for gender-responsive actions to be taken under each activity area of the project.
* Specific activities to ensure gender mainstreaming during the implementation and operation of the project.
* Specific performance indicators to measure and track progress on these actions at the activity level.


### 3.3 Design project implementation and monitoring institutional arrangements

* All gender outputs, outcomes and impact indicators will be incorporated into a detailed Monitoring & Evaluation plan, which will be developed during inception of the project.
* The MoU will provide concrete recommendations on how to ensure gender information continues to be collected and measured throughout implementation.


### 3.4 Estimate the implementation budgets

A gender-specific budget will be allocated for each project activity.

While conducting the plan, gender specialists in executing agencies are assigned to project teams with adequate resources to ensure GAP implementation.

Nongovernment organizations and community-based organizations contracted to implement activities will also be provided with adequate resources to build capacity.

Generally, the Gender Action Plan also is a complementary effort to the on-going and planned activities by local agricultural and Women's NGOs/CBOs in Gaza. These organizations are active in gender issues and women's empowerment and capacity building for agricultural programs.


### 4. Drivers of Change

The Gender Analysis Report identified four overarching systemic constraints to women in the project area: (1) adverse social norms; (2) discriminatory laws and lack of legal protection; (3) the financial exclusion of women, which is a result of gender-based discrimination and (4) lack of access to financial and property assets. Below are the recommended drivers of change for each constraint, with concrete actions and interventions that have shown to have


---
**Page 120**
---

a demonstrated impact1 in reducing gender inequality and are promising in the local context of Gaza. Some actions may help in more than one area.

### 4.1 Tackling adverse norms and promoting positive role models

Transforming the norms that limit women's access to work is core to achieving women’s economic empowerment. Social norms are the rules of behavior that are considered acceptable or appropriate for women and men; about women’s mobility outside the home; about the value of women’s work; about the justification for violence against women; and about women and men’s rights to resources, including land tenure and respect at work and equal access to property—all of these shape women's opportunities.

As part of this project, some interventions could include:

- The WUSA (or other association) that will handle the operation and maintenance of the recovery scheme must have robust female participation, including in leadership positions. Various ways of mandating female inclusion are discussed below.
- Provide specialized training on a variety of technical skills for irrigation and management of recovered water so that female farmers have the skills and information needed to make them eligible to do the task of irrigating in a professional way. Once women have these skills, they will be able to work with a broader array of options, adverse social norms regarding acceptable women’s work will be challenged and altered.
- The WUSA, or perhaps another office of PWA should help create a mentorship program between older, more experienced female farmers and younger ones to pass institutional knowledge and promote positive role models.

### 4.2 Ensuring legal protection and reforming discriminatory laws and regulations

Laws reflect a society’s expectations for gender roles. By guaranteeing equal opportunities and protections and by removing legal barriers, governments signal their commitment to achieve and enforce gender equality. Laws provide a powerful framework for women to know and assert their rights.

7 The United Nations Secretary-General's High-Level Panel on Women's Economic Empowerment has analyzed the impact of interventions in these areas and identified the programs that have the greatest demonstrable impact. See, Report of the UN Secretary-General’s High-Level Panel on Women’s Economic Empowerment, 2015.


---
**Page 121**
---

Although the political volatility of Palestine rules out any additions or amendments to the law (changing the WUA regulation to add a quota for women, for example), there are still actions that could improve conditions:

- Mandating female participation and leadership through the WUA bylaws (which have not yet been written);
- Encourage the creation of new or strengthening of existing female farmer organizations and CBSs;
- Carry out capacity building on various areas of the law that can educate and empower women, including on heritage and land ownership, contracts, and other areas.

### 4.3 Recognizing, reducing and redistributing unpaid work and care

Progress on the agenda to expand women's economic empowerment depends, to a significant extent, on reducing and redistributing the unpaid work and care that routinely consume a woman’s day. Only when the household work is reassigned can a woman spend more time in productive activities.

The reduction and redistribution of care require investments from both the public and private sector. These investments not only have major benefits for individuals and families but also major benefits for the economy, businesses and society at large, including increasing women's labor force participation in paid work, creating jobs in the care sector and strengthening the education of children, with beneficial effects for the development of their talent and future productivity.

The time spent on care can be reduced by investing in basic infrastructure and in time- and labor-saving technology. And it requires redistributing care through larger roles for men at home and in the workplace, as well as investments in quality healthcare services, early childhood education and other social care services. In Gaza, some immediate solutions may be found by fostering greater dialogue on this issue between women and within the community for context-specific arrangements.

As part of this project, some interventions could include:

- Encourage the creation of new or strengthening of existing female farmer organizations and CBSs. These organizations should hold discussion groups around "influence" issues and other topics relevant to local solutions, such as child-care agreements or a daycare-to-arrangement between families in the project area.
- Capacity building on finance and how to budget so that women can better equilibrate their time spent at home vs. outside work, and how best to balance her time in a way that she can afford.
- There are several trainings already planned as part of this project that are designed for women farmers (e.g., drip irrigation, area-based irrigation technology, better crop selection, a cheaper source of water, etc.). Women must be equally included in each of these trainings. Once the profit from farming is higher


---
**Page 122**
---

than the cost of leaving home, more women will be able to afford to do more productive work.

### Building assets — Financial and property

Eliminating gender disparities in work and in society depends on eliminating gender disparities in access to key assets. As found in the Gender Analysis Report, women in the project area have little or no formal ownership or vested interest in assets such as land ownership or household income.

As part of this project, some interventions could include:

- Carry out capacity building on various areas of the law that can educate and empower women, including on heritage and land ownership, contracts, and other areas.
- Irrigation projects often include land titling components. Opportunity exists here for example to ensure that women are included in the process of land titling either directly or to husbands and wives jointly, depending on the prevailing socio-agricultural context of the area. Understanding specific gender division of labor in the project and investigating the extent to which women participate in irrigation projects using participatory investigations and gender-disaggregated land surveys can help determine their access to and control over land.
- Gather more information about women who own land but do not manage it in order to better understand their constraints and how they may be addressed through the project.

### Improving public sector practices in employment and procurement

Beyond the government's role in determining the legal, institutional and policy environments that affect women’s economic opportunities, governments are major employers and procurers of goods and services. The power of governments in setting high standards for and exemplifying gender equality at work cannot be underestimated.

As part of this project, some interventions could include:

- **PIWA should** include among project objectives specific reference to increasing women's access to land ownership and irrigation projects and plan for ways to increase their access to productive resources.
- **PIWA needs** to have a better understanding of the social, economic, and institutional reality of its projects. In practical terms, this means that some modest increases in resources should be allocated for an assessment of such realities, particularly during the implementation and operation stages.
- The gender expert at PIWA could monitor progress and provide specialized training, thereafter identifying which interventions and financial incentives may be most effective in providing more opportunities for women.


---
**Page 123**
---

### 4.6 Strengthening visibility, collective voice and representation

Women’s organizations play a critical role in driving women’s economic opportunities. Women who belong to such organizations can benefit from access to finance, business and leadership skills, and technical advice and assistance, such as how to link to markets and supply chains.

The participation of women in Water Users Associations (WUA) allows women to voice their needs and demands, enhance their bargaining power, advocate for legal and policy reforms, and increase access to markets on fair and efficient terms. As part of this project, some interventions could include:

- Carry out capacity building on various areas of the law that can educate and empower women, including on heritage and land ownership, contracts, and other areas.
- The WUA or, perhaps, the government office of PAKO could help create a mentorship program for women who are experienced in farming practices.
- Promote the participation of women in WUAs and other organizations by supporting appropriate institutional measures, such as minimum quotas, or allowing that other forms of representation (e.g., proportional representation) be used.

Approaches for ensuring greater female membership in Water User Associations have included the following:

- Quota systems: Allocate a minimum number of board seats are reserved for women. This positive discrimination can increase women’s participation, though quotas have also backfired in other places or been “captured” by women put up to the position by dominant men. It is also important that women be given leadership roles and positions among membership ranks as well as leadership, so that a “critical mass” of women develops.
- Gender-inclusive WUAs: by removing exclusionary membership criteria regarding land ownership. This would change membership to WUAs so that it is not limited to farmers who own the land and, instead, would be associated with irrigation systems or water sources. Much depends here on how “farmer” is defined, for example, not just “irrigators” applying water to the field, which may be a man’s task in many places, but also other farmers who are involved in the management of water resources. In some cases, women are often women, and beyond crop production, those farmers using water for livestock production and other uses (often women).
- Scope for representation: Women can represent themselves at the community level that represent women's needs and interests, provided they use it formally to the WUA. Examples of such associations are cooperatives in which membership is not limited just to owners of land but to any type of tenure. Such associations may take the place


---
**Page 124**
---

of a traditional WUA or work in parallel with them. Recognizing organizational pluralism with various groups set up to respond to different needs is important.

# Implementation, Monitoring and follow up of gender-related targets and activities

The PWA will be responsible for the implementation of the GAP. A focal person and Coordinator of the GAP is designated in the PWA team in Gaza. The GAP Coordinator will be assisted by the TA Gender Expert and related expertise and will coordinate closely with FAO for gender related activities. The GAP Coordinator will also be in charge of leading the mainstreaming of gender issues within the PWA local staff in Gaza and Ramallah.

The PWA will be assisted in the implementation of the Gender Action Plan by the provision of specialized Gender expertise through the Technical Assistance services that are funded under the project – the expertise will include provision of training of PWA staff and mainstreaming of gender into all aspects of the project. The GAP Coordinator will also assist in ensuring that the project provides access to water for women, the FAO will be an important partner for the implementation of the GAP. In terms of methodology, it will be implemented using inputs from the FAO (see Annex 8 of the GCF Funding Proposal) and particularly from the International Union for Conservation of Nature- IUCN’s Training Manual on Gender and Climate Change12.

Systematic follow-up is needed to ensure that policy reforms and the GAP is implemented. Monitoring is the continuous collection and analysis of information to track progress. The monitoring mechanism, plan and tools should be defined from inception and be ongoing. Monitoring should clearly define what will be measured, by whom, with what quality and frequency. It should also identify the resources required for one of the project and the associated budget. Gender sensitive indicators both quantitative and qualitative should assess changes pertaining to gender indicators. M&E approach necessary to ensure effective implementation of this gender action plan, and will provide performance targets to allow periodic monitoring of progress towards meeting the Plan’s overarching goals. The periodic monitoring and reporting will also contribute to the Plan’s review process. The M&E plan should be flexible enough to allow for learning and taking up of the most successful initiatives. The M&E plan should be able to identify challenges and feasible solutions in order to readjust any action that might be reinforcing discriminatory social norms or expose beneficiaries to violence, in respect of the do no harm principle.

It is very important to enhance ownership through internal participatory monitoring especially while analysis related to promotion of gender equality. The GAP will include sufficient budget allocation for implementation and gender capacity building for WUA and Women CBOs as partners in the targeted area. # Gender Baseline Study

## 1. Introduction

This Gender Analysis Report aims to assess the implications of this project’s planned activities in order to identify vulnerabilities or disadvantages of women that the project is in a position to improve. By utilizing the baseline survey and other data, this report seeks to determine how the project can respond to the needs of women and men in view of the specific climate change issues to be addressed.

Gender analysis in the study refers to different roles, rights, responsibilities of men and women and the relations between them. Gender does not simply refer to women or men, but to the way their qualities, behaviors, and identities are determined through the process of socialization. The different positions of women and men are influenced by historical, religious, economic, and cultural factors.

Gender relations are often dictated by unequal power dynamics that assign particular roles, determine access to decision-making and access to and control over resources. Part of this Gender Analysis Report, therefore, is to understand the inequalities between women and men that influence their ability to access water in different areas. In some areas it will be the guardians of and main users of water while men are the providers and managers of water resources. Understanding gender roles, relations, and inequalities can help to explain the choices male and female make, their options, and how best to foster greater social equity.

## 2. Water situation in Gaza Strip

In Gaza Strip, 95% of households are connected to the domestic water network, which is not suitable for drinking or cooking (68.4% of water resources in Gaza are contaminated). Drinking water is obtained mainly from private vendors who drive it directly to homes. In some areas children are in charge of getting water from the closest desalination plant or filling “SAHEL” point (which is free of charge); in some Bedouin communities, women bring drinking water on a donkey cart.

Less than 60% of households are connected to a wastewater network. The best situation is in the northern governorate where 87.4% HH are connected to wastewater networks1.

The population in the Gaza Strip receives just 5-8 hours of water supply every 3 days2.


1. **Wash assessment at household level in Gaza strip (UNICEF, GVA, and PMA)**
2. **UN - Women and GVA, 2016. “Gender & Wash hot hits for Palestine”.**

3. WHY MAINSTREAM GENDER IN WASH IN PALESTINE?

A gender approach to water and sanitation services aims to ensure that all people benefit from and are empowered by improved water and sanitation services that take into consideration different needs and preferences of men and women, as well as their meaningful participations for both males and females. The importance of involving both women and men in the management of water has been recognized at the global level as not only intrinsically desirable but also practically beneficial – involving women in water projects increases project effectiveness and sustainability.

Use of water is one of the major challenges in overcoming gender inequalities. Inadequate access to safe, hygienic and private sanitation facilities is a source of shame, physical discomfort and insecurity for millions of women across the world. Access to use of recovered water for irrigation is also inequitable, the issue we work on in this study.

Ensuring women participation is quite challenging in the specific context of Palestine. Often gender inequalities are seen as a lack of capacity or WASH actors to ensure women participation is wrongly attributed to "cultural restrictions and norms" and results in a lack of gender equity in WASH and recovered water services.


j UN - Women and GIZ, 2016. "Gender & WASH tool kit for Palestine".

# 4. LITERATURE REVIEW

There is a scarcity of studies on gender mainstreaming in the WASH sector, especially for treated wastewater in Palestine. To gather information, the WASH cluster at UNDP, Women Institute Studies in BZU, PWA (Gaza & West Bank), UN-Women, and GCF’s website and other sources were consulted. Four relevant studies were reviewed, as follows:

## A. The first study by Hanadi Badr (2013)

It is a master thesis of a student at Birzeit University – Women Institute Studies, who was studying Gender & Development. The title of the study: “Women Empowerment Towards Treated Wastewater Reuse: Resonant Practical & Strategic Needs in Palestine.”

### The issue treated wastewater is suggested as a solution that might partially resolve the domestic water crisis as well as protect the environment and food security. Some people attributed the water deficit to climate change, others attribute it to the lack of justice in the distribution of water resources or result of conflict over water.

Main objective of the study is to assess the impact of treating Palestinian rural women on the reuse of treated wastewater. Determining whether having access to greater information about wastewater would increase their acceptance of its use. It studied 120 women, divided into two groups of 60, who were given 10 days of training on treated wastewater use. The acceptance of wastewater for drinking water increased from 3% in the control group to 54% in the training (the acceptance rate for the control group that did not receive training did not change).

## B. The second study by Marwan Ghannam (2015)

Titled “Gender Empowerment and Treated Wastewater Reuse in Ein Qiniya Village, Ramallah – Palestine.” The paper aims to improve the empowerment of communities and individuals—particularly women on the acceptance of wastewater reuse for productive purposes. A survey was conducted among 30 households located at Al-Ati-Tireh camp and EinQiniya village in Ramallah about whether or not training women in treated wastewater use can increase their awareness and acceptance regarding the reuse of treated wastewater from the Ain Qiniya Wastewater Treatment Plant.

A roughly selected sample of 30 households from Ein Qiniya inhabitants was interviewed. Women’s empowerment was analyzed using Alkire methodology that illustrates five domains of women empowerment: (1) production, (2) decision making power over productive resources; (3) control over use of income, (4) leadership in the community, and (5) time use [Alkire, 2012]. These domains were used in recent study to assess women empowerment in Ein Qiniya.

65% of the study sample respondents did not have a problem in the use of wastewater, while 35% had no objection to the idea of wastewater reuse. 55% of the study sample were convinced to use waste water, 30% agree to use it, but only 15% disagree to use the treated wastewater. The acceptance of individuals to use treated wastewater for drinking shows that


---
**Page 147**
---

only 35% agree to use it for drinking, 15% disagree, and 50% are not sure of the extent of their acceptance of the idea. As for the reasons that result in non-acceptance of the idea of water reuse were as follows: 70% answered that the reason is a health, 23% answered that it is cultural, and 7% say it is for personal reasons.

C. The third Study by GVC - Italian Cooperation (2017) titled "Gender & Wash Toolkit for Palestine".

The study aims to mainstream gender in WASH projects in Palestine (Gaza & West). A gender approach to water and sanitation services aims to ensure that all people benefit from and are empowered by improved water & sanitation services and hygiene practices that take into consideration the different needs of genders.

To mainstream gender in WASH projects in Palestine, the study Conducted a gender analysis and collect sex and age disaggregated data (SAAD) in order to form an accurate and nuanced picture of the situation of target communities and personal/social relations within them. The study team ensure that the design of interventions addresses gender needs and integrates gender considerations and include meaningful participation in the formulation of the intervention by different groups of the population (e.g., women, persons with disabilities, adolescent girls, farmers, etc.).

Design a Monitoring and Evaluation(M&E) plan at the very beginning of the project with appropriate indicators that will measure the gendered impact of activities and the achievement or lack of expected results and objectives. To link the actions of the implementation with the gender analysis.

The study clarifies the unequal relationships between women and men, girls and boys and characterized by a differentiation of roles, affecting and influencing all areas and spheres of life. Gender relations are often dictated by unequal power dynamics that assign particular roles, determine access to decision making and access/control over resources. Access to water, sanitation facilities (WASH) is often determined and controlled primarily by gender relations and roles. In all the world including Palestine; WASH facilities and access to water is associated with responsibilities undertaken by women, since they are the ones responsible inside the home for cleaning, cooking, washing clothes and dishes. The availability of WASH facilities in education and health facilities has significant gender differentiated impact. Access to water for agricultural use also has significant implications for women working in agriculture.

Gender considerations in securing WASH needs during humanitarian emergencies and in long term interventions ensures protection of women and girls from Gender Based Violence (GBV). To improve the conditions of women in different societies, several guidelines have been developed worldwide aiming to provide direction and recommendations for gender mainstreaming in water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) projects. Societal and political


---
**Page 148**
---

constraints in the Palestinian context make gender mainstreaming in WASH projects a challenging task. The toolkit provides technical instruments to tackle some of the most significant issues affecting women in the WASH sector in Palestine.

D. The Fourth Study is a manual prepared by GCF and UN Women titled "Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Fund Projects", GCF senior staff and Mr. Sub Ghosal (2017)

This is a practical guide for integrating considerations of gender equality in climate change interventions. The manual addresses the GCF's potential to mainstream gender into climate finance. Gender mainstreaming is central to GCF's objectives and guiding principles through engaging women and men of all ages as stakeholders in the design, development, and implementation.

Gender equality considerations should be mainstreamed into the entire project cycle to enhance the efficiency of climate change mitigation and adaptation interventions and ensure that the gender co-benefits are obtained. Gender mainstreaming is fundamental to any project intervention to make climate interventions more effective and efficient.

The manual is based on a one-day training on gender delivered by UN Women. The GCF requested UN Women to develop this manual as part of its integration of gender equality concerns in climate change interventions and investments. The manual reviews GCF gender policy and related programming provisions, including readiness support. It covers a number of gender mainstreaming tools and methodological approaches, which can be employed in designed projects, such as gender analysis, gender assessment and action plan, and gender responsive results or logical frameworks. The manual focuses on the minimum set of tools needed to meet the project cycle.

# GENDER ROLES

As a first mandatory step, let us briefly define some basic key concepts:

* **Gender:** Refers to how societies and specific cultures assign roles and ascribe characteristics to men and women on the basis of their sex; refers to the socially constructed roles, behaviors, activities, and attributes that a given society considers suitable for women and men of different ages. Gender is a series of constructed categories and therefore can change and evolve over time.
* **Sex:** Refers to refers to the biological, physiological and anatomical characteristics that define men and women.
* **Gender roles:** Gender roles are theoretical constructs used for social analysis. A role reflects a model or pattern for an individual, including certain rights and duties. A role also relates to a certain status and social power. The role of an individual changes according to social and cultural changes in society.
* **Gender-based division of labor:** A division of labor according to social and cultural patterns that define the roles of men and women, boys and girls both inside and outside the family, and separate from their biological differences between men and women.
* **Gender equality** refers to equal rights, powers, responsibilities, and opportunities for women and men, as well as taking into consideration the needs and priorities of women and men.
* **Gender equity:** Refers to the ability of being fair to women and men to reduce disparity of social disadvantages that prevent women and men from operating on an equal basis. It is based on the principle that both women and men of all ages and abilities have the right to "access opportunities" to improve their quality of life - individually and collectively.
* **Gender Sensitivity:** Refers to the understanding of the ways that people think about gender and socio-cultural factors underlying gender inequality.
* **Gender mainstreaming:** Gender mainstreaming is the concept of assessing the different implications for women and men of any planned policy action or project in all areas and levels.

In this study, we study the baseline data to get an understanding of gender roles, gender needs, and access to resources (including recovered water) for irrigation use by the beneficiaries in Gaza city and Gaza North area. This Report seeks to identify restrictions that female farmers face in order to see how the project may reduce any existing vulnerabilities or disadvantages to women, in the context of the project's goals and activities.


---
**Page 150**
---

# Methodology

The methodology includes qualitative and quantitative techniques.

For quantitative data, a random selection of 106 farmers in the targeted area were selected with near-equal participation among the sexes (51 Females, 55 Males) for a total of 106 participants. The questionnaire survey aimed to understand the status of women in terms of ownership of land and livestock, access to credit, membership in cooperatives or marketing groups, membership in agricultural institutions, and access to training. Field workers who filled the questionnaires entered the answers into an SPSS spreadsheet; then answers were analyzed.

Another quantitative tool used for this Report is focus groups. We held 6 focus group discussions in the targeted area: 2 in Jabalia; 2 in Beit Lahia at Shehara area; and 2 in Beit Hanoun. The focus groups included women, men, young people (18-30 years), girls (15-17 years), and people with disabilities (PwDs) so that they could express their feelings freely. The researcher facilitated the sessions with a co-facilitator. The sessions were recorded via mobile phone and the co-facilitator wrote benchmarks of the discussion.

For qualitative information, discussions were conducted with two gender specialists in PWA: one from PWA in Jabalia as she is gender focal point; and another from PWA in Gaza as she is the gender focal point in the project department. Gender analysis done in accordance of GCF policy and gender theories as well as the PWA policy and priorities to mainstream gender.


---
**Page 151**
---

7. Geography of the Study Area

The Palestinian Water Authority (PWA) is executing the Northern Gaza Emergency Sewage Treatment (NGEST) Project initiated in 2004. Around 15,000 dunums of agricultural lands are located in the eastern part of North Gaza and Gaza City that will be irrigated by recovery water wells from the NGEST project. Farmers are either owners or workers at these lands living in North Gaza (Ben Hanoun, Jabalia, and Gaza city). This area is in the buffer zone near the border to Israel and Egypt. The region has a high vulnerability to climate change compounded with the effects of climate change and increasing aridity and overall water insecurity.

The project's goal is to develop an integrated and low-emission water management scheme capable of mitigating the impact of increasing aridity due to climate change, by delivering water in quality and quantity for sustaining agriculture and preserving the health of highly vulnerable populations on the Gaza Strip. The project was prepared with no specific approach to tackle gender issues, so this study was commissioned as a complementary study to mainstream gender considerations into the project.

8. Recovered groundwater and sociality

Population growth and rapid urbanization are intensifying pressure on freshwater resources in Gaza Strip as tests show that 96.4% of domestic water resources are contaminated. The NGEST projects seeks to reduce the pressure on freshwater use in Northern Gaza by creating a "new" water source, which will be used for irrigation. Overall, the analysis of the answers from the questionnaires shows that 98.6% of the respondents (farmers) agree to use this recovered water for irrigation; 95.4 Male 98% Female Agree to use the non-conventional water for irrigation.

Women awareness around water reuse is considered a strategic need that can empower because it affects traditional women roles (Bader 2013). Investing in women training can increase their awareness of the issues of reuse of treated wastewater and their acceptance of reusing this water. The importance of creating knowledge and awareness toward recovered water for agricultural activities, even for communities that have water networks, becomes a strategic need for all Palestinian men and women.

Analyzing the surveys of respondents from the sample that of 106, 19 males answered yes that they received training on related topics, and 35 farmers answered no. For female farmers, 8 of the respondents received training and 43 females did not have training on agricultural issues.


### Gender & Recovered water in Palestine

Few studies have been conducted in Palestine that connect gender, water, and treated wastewater. Water quality that is an indicator to gender equity was studied by Bader (2013). Women roles in rural areas are generally limited to subsistence farming methods which limits that Palestinian women are prohibited from gaining the necessary knowledge to carry out these roles in the safest and most productive way. Bader in her study concluded that involving women in environmental education will be important regarding sustainable water management.

The WASH sector is a technical sector which needs technical tools to be able to intervene administratively. If you have a treatment plant and you do not have the ability, know-how and skills to deal with this component, there will be no administration. If the female farmer has the knowledge, she will become specialized and she will be more active in the sector.

9. Gender Analysis

### 9.1 Questionnaire Analyses

A randomly selected sample of 106 farmers (51 females, 55 males) in the project area were interviewed. The questionnaire focused on the socio-economic situation, roles (productive & reproductive), and access to resources (land and water). It also inquired about any past training on agricultural or related topics, and about acceptance of using recovered water for irrigation.

#### Statistical Analysis

The data collected from field observations and workshop participants was analysed and presented respectively. The data collected by survey was analyzed using SPSS (Statistical Package for Social Science) program.

### 9.1.1 Socio-economic situation

The targeted area in the northern area in Gaza (Isbaha, Beit Hanoun, and Shijaiya area at Gaza City) near the border, the population is very poor, with high unemployment rate, low household incomes, a high cost of living (particularly for food) and the erosion of livelihoods have resulted in continued high levels of food insecurity in Gaza Strip. Access to essential services such as health care and education are also limited in this area compared to other areas, as unemployment rate is higher in this area than other areas (72.5 % unemployment rate), as questionnaires’ results show. The survey shows that most of the women spend their time in the household doing domestic work and follow traditional roles, and males spend most of their time in the field.

Over (86.8%) of the sample depended on the male in the family as the breadwinner for the household’s income. Moreover, the majority of the respondents (72.5%) were not employed, indicating that most were housewives and depended on their spouses for their income.

The household source of income is from different sectors, but from agricultural production is the highest percentage 65.1% (Figure 2).


---
**Page 154**
---

Figure (2) Family income source

The majority of farmers had seasonal income, ranging from 1200 - 12,000 NIS from agricultural activities as shown in Figure (3). Olive oil production is the highest income compared to the income from other crops.

83% of the sample cultivate privately owned land, while 17% work on land that is rented. Also results indicated that expenditures, notably input costs, impact the decisions made by the respondents in terms of production including the type of crops grown. 84% of males respondents and 90% of females confirmed they have easy access to water for irrigation.

The decisions about land and other resources were dominated by men in most cases as the results illustrated that the male in the household, being either the husband or the father, makes the decision on how income is spent.


### 9.1.2 Current Water Sources for Irrigation

As estimated 53.3% of the population in the targeted area irrigates their lands from collective wells, 20% from private wells, 6.6% from rainwater. To irrigate from wells, either collective or private, is expensive; it costs them around 60 NIS per hour yet the income from the farm and agricultural production does not cover this cost.

The majority of respondents agree to use the recovered water for irrigation purposes, and they believe this recovered water will contribute to the solution of water scarcity and will positively impact the environment. This indicates that people had the desire to use recovered water and have a positive attitude towards it. 98% of the sample (about 75%) has background information of NGEST and the project. 98.6% agree to be connected to the network of this recovered water for irrigation, just 1.4% did not agree.

### Control/decision over production crops

Respondents confirmed that decisions related to agricultural work are made by the male in a high percentage of cases (about 80%), while in only 15-20% of the cases is the female involved. In some tasks, such as harvesting and seeding, the percentage is increased, but for fertilization and irrigation the percentage decreased (Table 1).

Although these results suggest that females are not as empowered as males to make decisions for the household in agricultural matters, it may be more nuanced than that. 'Statistical' perspectives on decision-making should be remembered for what they are: simple windows on complex realities. They may provide a brief glimpse into processes of decision-making, but they tell us very little about the informal networks that go between households and their private lives. Consequently, you may underestimate the informal decision-making agency which women often exercise. As pointed by Kabir (1999), women may opt for 'private' forms of empowerment to protect their family image, and honor of the traditional decision-maker but which nevertheless increases women's 'backstage' influence in decision-making processes" (Bassu, 1996; Chen, 1983; Kabir, 1997).

10. Climate change and agricultural activities

Men and women both noticed significant climate changes during the last decade. Around 47% of males had noticed changes in temperature and rainfall during the last decade while around 38% of the females had notices these changes. Therefore, around 45% farmers confirmed significant impacts on their agricultural activities at the project area while 33% of females agreed. Accordingly, around 11.4% of male farmers had changed their crops while only 6.7% of females agreed on changing the crops. The decision of changing the crop is dominated by male farmers, their sons and the labourers.

### Focus Group Discussions Analysis:

Six workshops were conducted with farmers from local communities in the targeted area (Table 2). At least 10 farmers in every workshop; 10 females and 10 males except in one area of the targeted area. The main issue discussed at the meeting raised in the workshops is wastewater management and use of recovered water for irrigation from NGST plant. Six focus group discussions conducted during field research in Gaza raised that it is prevented to cultivate trees there or any kind of plants taller that 1 meter for security reasons in Jabalia, Beit Hanoun, & Shejaihe at Gaza City. They cultivate just seasonal plants.

### Dialogue about use of recovered water

In a group discussion with female farmers in Beit Hanoun, most are members in Saving & Lending Cooperative Society in Beit Hanoun. Some of the participants were members in other societies but none of them visited to agriculture and food processing in Shijaiya areas of Gaza City, no female was a member in any society, the same in Jabalia for membership. Female farmers in the Beit Hanoun discussion reflected that they accept to use recovered water for irrigation if it is cheaper than the one they are using from wells, as the electricity short feeding in el Gallon and the cost of pumping water from the well is high compared with recovered water. It is costly, one of the participants said: "El Baiara Majheet Gah Maiteha", which means cultivation of the farm did not cover the price of the water. This shows us that the farmers are suffering and they do not have any profit from farming – they just cover their house from cultivated crops.
